### Page 9

so much about creating additional investment as redirecting
investment from elsewhere in the economy.
What would US growth look like without AI? Not simply 37% lower.
Part of the capital, engineers and electricity absorbed by AI would
have found other uses. A world without the AI boom would most
likely show weaker growth, but not by the full amount of AI's
contribution – and with a more balanced investment mix.
For 2027, our plateau base case implies a smaller AI contribution to

growth. That would give the non-tech economy room to breathe –
but only if it is ready to take over.
For now, AI is a capex story, not a consumer story. And capex
stories have a habit of ending with a hangover.
The productivity question: when, if ever,
does AI show up in the statistics?
In the US and China, it already does – partly. The big gains are still
to come, and may arrive abruptly.
It depends on where you look. In the US, labour productivity has

picked up markedly since the start of the AI boom. Part of that
comes from high-skilled services, exactly where you would expect
AI to show up ﬁrst. But part of it reﬂects the enormous data centre
investments, which are capital-heavy and need very little labour.
Total factor productivity – the best available measure of gains from
technological progress – has improved decently in the US, but
without an exceptional acceleration. While AI-related sectors are

providing a signiﬁcant boost to several Asian economies, this
largely reﬂects rapid investment and higher capital utilisation
rather than a sustained improvement in underlying productivity.
However, in China, we’ve seen labour productivity growth pick up in
the last two years, breaking what had been an extended downtrend
as Chinese companies race to incorporate AI in workﬂows. In the
eurozone, which lacks comparable AI investment, labour
productivity growth remains lacklustre, with very few exceptions,

like for example, in the Netherlands.

### Page 10

The technology holds real promise for broad-based productivity
gains. But reaping them requires wider adoption and investment in
infrastructure and skills, both within companies and across the
economy. That suggests a J-curve: heavy upfront intangible
investment – time spent learning new skills and reorganising
workﬂows – could initially mute measured productivity before the
investment pays oﬀ, possibly quite abruptly. Electriﬁcation is the
classic precedent: the electric motor arrived in the 1880s, but

factory productivity only took oﬀ in the 1920s, once factories had
been rebuilt around it.
And even then, a shift of the workforce towards sectors where
productivity gains are harder to achieve – think healthcare and care
work – would dampen the macroeconomic eﬀect. Measurement
doesn't help either: when AI makes a free digital service better, GDP
statistics barely notice.
Still, after decades of slowing productivity growth, AI is likely to be a
game changer. The question is not so much whether it will show up

in the statistics, but when. In 1987, Robert Solow quipped that you
could see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity
statistics. It took about a decade to prove him wrong.
Is AI inﬂationary or deﬂationary?
Electricity, memory chips and construction
costs point one way; long-run efﬁciency
gains point the other.
Inﬂationary today, disinﬂationary tomorrow – but don't hold your
breath for the outright deﬂation Silicon Valley promises.
It depends on your time horizon. For now, the eﬀect runs in the

inﬂationary direction. AI's enormous energy demand is adding to
the current energy shock, memory chip prices have surged, and the
scramble for electricians, transformers and construction capacity is
pushing up costs. Meanwhile, the investment boom is pouring fuel
on the ﬁre of a US economy already running with above-target
inﬂation.

### Page 11

In the longer run, once the technology lifts productivity, it should
lower unit labour costs and exert disinﬂationary pressure across the
economy. Like most technological breakthroughs, AI will probably
end up being disinﬂationary. But the eﬀect will be concentrated in
services and much smaller for goods with physical inputs.
Algorithms can't make steel, copper or concrete cheaper.
There is also a less comfortable twist. AI allows companies to price

dynamically, adjusting prices to what each customer is willing to
pay and thereby capturing more of the consumer surplus. That
could make some prices stickier rather than lower. Together with
the physical constraints, this makes the outright deﬂation Sam
Altman envisages rather unlikely.
For AI itself, the picture is diﬀerent. As we argued in 2024, we expect
a downward price trend for AI services, as with other software
categories, and at the sector level we continue to hold that view.

Competition between model providers should keep the pressure on.
For central banks, this means the AI disinﬂation dividend is a story
for the second half of the decade, not for 2027. Until then, the
boom adds to demand faster than it adds to supply – the textbook
recipe for inﬂation, not deﬂation.
How is AI reshaping global trade and
investment ﬂows, from Taiwanese chips to
Dutch lithography?
AI is redrawing global trade around a single supply chain – and

rewarding the countries that own its choke points.
AI is increasingly sorting the global economy into winners and
laggards. The IMF has raised its outlook for economies beneﬁting
from the AI investment boom, particularly those embedded in the
global technology supply chain, while countries outside it see
much less of a boost. Taiwan is riding booming semiconductor
demand, South Korea is beneﬁting from demand for high-
bandwidth memory, and the eﬀects travel upstream to the

suppliers of advanced chipmaking equipment, including the
Netherlands.

### Page 12

This illustrates how AI is reshaping both trade and investment
ﬂows. Spending on data centres and computing capacity in the US
drives demand for chips from Asia, which in turn requires
investment in semiconductor capacity and equipment from Europe,
Japan and the US. The result is an international AI investment
chain, with growth increasingly ﬂowing to the countries that hold
its critical links. It also explains a good part of the surge in the US
import bill: every data centre built in Texas shows up in Taiwan's

export statistics.
Trade policy is the second force reshaping the chain. Export controls
on advanced chips to China, tariﬀs and subsidy programmes such
as the US Chips Act (or direct government stakes in companies) are
pushing companies to build capacity closer to home or in friendly
countries. That makes the chain more resilient on paper, but also
more expensive – another reason why the deﬂationary promise of
AI may take longer to materialise.
The ﬂip side is concentration risk. If the chain snaps – through a

demand slump, an escalation of export controls or a Taiwan crisis –
the countries beneﬁting most today would feel it ﬁrst and hardest.
What does AI mean for labour markets, and
for how central banks read them?
So far, AI is reshaping who gets hired rather than how many are
employed – which makes it a real-time headache for central
banks.
Evidence on AI's labour market impact remains fragmented, and
the economy-wide eﬀect is still limited. If history is any guide, fears

of mass unemployment are overdone: with every major technology
of past centuries, growth was strong enough to create jobs
elsewhere, even as demand for some types of labour fell. But if
adoption is so fast that labour demand and supply can't adjust in
time, unemployment could rise temporarily.
The ﬁrst cracks are showing at the entry level. In the US, job growth
has remained positive but weaker in sectors with high AI exposure;
evidence from the eurozone is mixed. The Dallas Fed ﬁnds that

### Page 13

employment among young workers in AI-exposed occupations has
weakened, mainly reﬂecting lower hiring rather than more layoﬀs.
According to the Stanford Digital Economy Lab, employment
among 22-25-year-olds in highly exposed occupations was around
19% below where it would have been by June 2026 had it kept
pace with less-exposed occupations. In China, we see similar
pressures on entry-level work reﬂected in rising youth
unemployment. The Chinese government has planned training and

job replacement policies to help alleviate any negative eﬀects of AI
on employment. For developing Asia, particularly India and the
Philippines where outsourcing sectors are highly exposed to AI, the
evidence points to adaptation rather than decline. AI is changing
the nature of outsourced work, not reducing demand for it.
AI is not ﬁring people; it is quietly not hiring them. Still, none of this
points to mass displacement. Yet. And with ageing populations and

persistent labour shortages, the real question may be whether
shortages or AI turn out to be the bigger problem.
For central banks, AI is less of a short-run issue than you might
expect. Higher productivity growth combined with more muted job
growth could be the healthy sign of an economy absorbing a new
technology, and come with more moderate inﬂation. The tricky
part is reading it in real time. A soft payrolls print could signal either
a weakening economy or a more productive one – and the right

policy response would be very diﬀerent. Get it wrong, and a central
bank either cuts rates into a productivity boom or sits on its hands
as the economy weakens.
Who pays?
Is the boom still funded from cash ﬂows, or
are hyperscalers increasingly turning to
debt?
Still mostly from cash ﬂows – but the cushion is thinning and the
borrowing has begun.

### Page 14

The largest hyperscalers have so far paid for the AI build-out out of
their own pockets, which is the biggest diﬀerence from the dotcom
and railway booms. But capex is absorbing a rising share of
operating cash ﬂow, squeezing free cash ﬂow and leaving less room
for share buybacks. For companies that used to return cash to
shareholders at a record pace, that is a notable shift in priorities.
Borrowing is stepping up. Several hyperscalers, such as Amazon,

Alphabet, Meta, and Oracle have issued jumbo bond deals since
2025, with Oracle the most leveraged of the group. Equity markets
are being tapped too: several big tech ﬁrms announced plans to
raise equity in June. None of this is alarming on its own – these are
investment-grade companies with strong balance sheets – but it
marks a change of phase. Cash-funded booms are self-limiting:
they stop when the cash runs out. Debt-funded booms can run
further, and fall harder.

The key is to distinguish between the players. The cash-rich
hyperscalers can absorb a disappointment in AI returns; their core
businesses in advertising, cloud and software keep generating cash
regardless. The neoclouds and AI labs are a diﬀerent story. They
depend entirely on outside money – venture capital, private credit,
vendor ﬁnancing and strategic investors – and their business
models only work if revenue keeps growing spectacularly. They are

the most likely point of stress if ﬁnancing conditions tighten.
In between sits a growing grey zone of leases, joint ventures and
special purpose vehicles that keep debt oﬀ the hyperscalers' own
books.
The direction of travel is clear. The boom started as a self-funded
venture by the richest companies in the world. It is gradually
turning into a credit story.
Leases, SPVs, private credit: how opaque
has AI ﬁnancing become?
More opaque than the hyperscalers' balance sheets suggest – but

so far, the risk rests on some of the strongest borrowers in the
world.

### Page 15

A growing part of AI ﬁnancing is moving oﬀ balance sheet. Special
purpose vehicles and joint ventures, such as Meta's Hyperion data
centre ﬁnanced with Blue Owl, long-term leases and GPU-backed
loans allow companies to fund the build-out without the debt
appearing directly on their own books. Securitisation of data centre
assets through asset-backed securities and commercial mortgage-
backed securities is growing as well, bringing a wider pool of

investors into the game.
Leases are a particular blind spot. They are recognised as on-
balance-sheet commitments only once contracts are ﬁnal; until
then, the extent of incremental lease liabilities is uncertain. There is
also the question of who carries the risk if the tenant walks away
after the initial term. Although initial lease terms of 15 years are
not uncommon, these can still be shorter than the economic life of
the underlying infrastructure, leaving some residual risk around

renewal or re-leasing.
That said, risk has to be assessed contract by contract. The largest
technology ﬁrms, with very solid balance sheets, are well placed to
take on additional liabilities, and much of the oﬀ-balance-sheet
structure ultimately rests on their creditworthiness. The problem is
less hidden insolvency than hidden concentration: it is hard to see
how much of the system leans on the same handful of tenants.
Opacity also complicates the macro picture. When ﬁnancing is

spread across SPVs, private credit funds and securitisations, it
becomes harder for investors, regulators and central banks to see
where risks are concentrated – and harder to judge how a shock
would travel.
Looking ahead, capital may eventually become a major constraint
on the AI build-out, alongside permitting and access to power. The
more the industry relies on creative ﬁnancing, the more it depends
on investors' continued willingness to lend. Financial engineering

can prolong a boom, but it can't make the returns appear.
How exposed are banks, and is private
credit the shadow banking of this cycle?

### Page 16

Direct bank exposure looks modest; private credit is the obvious
weak spot – but contagion would run through markets rather than
bank failures.
Banks' direct exposure to AI looks limited. The hyperscalers fund
themselves mainly through cash ﬂow and bond markets rather
than bank loans. Indirect exposure is harder to gauge: banks lend to
private credit funds, data centre developers and construction ﬁrms.
Private credit is the obvious weak spot. Is it the shadow banking of

this cycle? Partly. It has less of the maturity mismatch that made
pre-2008 shadow banking so fragile: much of the capital is locked
up in closed-end funds, so investors can't run. But it shares other
features: opacity, infrequent valuation marks and leverage on
leverage, with funds borrowing from banks to lend to borrowers
that are themselves highly leveraged. And it is increasingly exposed
to AI through loans to neoclouds, GPU-backed ﬁnancing and data

centre developers.
The pressure point is the semi-liquid vehicles sold to retail and
wealth clients. They promise periodic liquidity on assets that are
anything but liquid. In a downturn, redemption requests could force
managers to impose gates, damaging conﬁdence across the asset
class. So far, these vehicles are not core to the AI build-out, but they
are where a shock could turn into a story.
If stress does materialise, we would expect contagion to run

through tighter ﬁnancial conditions and wider credit spreads across
the economy rather than through bank failures. That is less
dramatic than 2008, but not harmless: a repricing of credit risk
would raise funding costs far beyond tech, hitting the very non-tech
investment that is already struggling.
Banks may well escape an AI bust largely unscathed. The economy
they lend to may not.
Vendor ﬁnancing déjà vu: should we worry
when chipmakers fund their own

customers?

### Page 17

Worth watching, but this is not Lucent and Nortel all over again –
mainly because the balance sheets are much stronger.
The dotcom precedent is uncomfortable. Lucent, Nortel and Cisco
lent money to telecom start-ups so they could buy their
equipment. When the customers went bust, the suppliers took the
hit, and much of the reported demand turned out to have been
ﬁnanced by the sellers themselves.
Today, there are echoes. Chipmakers are taking stakes in AI labs

and neoclouds that then buy their chips. The same handful of ﬁrms
raise money, buy chips, lease compute and book revenue oﬀ one
another. The concern is less solvency than signal: circular deals
inﬂate reported demand and blur true end-demand. Revenue that
only exists because the supplier paid for it isn't demand.
But the picture diﬀers in important ways. Many of these contracts
appear to be normal commercial arrangements, and AI labs also
generate fast-growing revenues from customers outside these

related deals. AI-related revenues are still only part of the much
larger businesses of the hyperscalers. And the macro backdrop is
very diﬀerent: the overall US corporate debt-to-GDP ratio is not
worrisome, in stark contrast to the leverage that built up before the
dotcom bust and the global ﬁnancial crisis. Circularity in a cash-rich
system is a transparency problem; circularity in a leveraged system
is a solvency problem.
That does not mean investors can relax. Circular deals create

concentration risk: if one key AI lab stumbles, the shock would
travel quickly through its suppliers, landlords and investors, who are
often the same companies. And they make it harder to judge how
much genuine end-demand there is for AI – precisely the question
on which the whole boom rests.
The test is simple: follow the money until it reaches a customer
who isn't also an investor. The longer that trail gets, the more
nervous we should become.
Higher Fed rates helped end the dotcom

bubble. Could monetary policy or bond

### Page 18

yields pop this one too?
Probably not as the trigger – but monetary policy could make any
bust more painful, because the safety net is thinner than in 2001.
In 1999-2000, the Fed hiked rates to 6.5% before the dotcom
bubble burst, then cut by almost ﬁve percentage points in 2001.
This time, an energy shock is keeping inﬂation above target. Actual
rate hikes and more rate hikes to come could bring both the Federal
Reserve and European Central Bank closer to risky territory.

High policy rates do not necessarily stop AI investment ﬁrst. If
expected returns on AI remain very high, capital may keep ﬂowing
in while ﬁnancing becomes uneconomic for lower-return
investment elsewhere. Tight money could deepen the split between
tech and non-tech investment rather than end the boom.
At the moment, we expect hyperscaler funding needs to increase
further as investment in AI infrastructure, data centres and power
capacity accelerates. We forecast total issuance of around

US$280bn in 2027, including approximately US$190bn in USD and
US$65bn in EUR (€60bn). For European credit markets, we estimate
a net TMT supply number of €103bn, which includes US$65bn
coming from hyperscalers.
The long end of the yield curve matters more than the policy rate.
As the AI build-out becomes increasingly debt-funded, higher bond
yields and term premia raise the cost of ﬁnancing long-term
investments, from data centres to power plants. A sustained rise in

long-term yields would therefore hit hardest where the build-out
depends most on external funding.
The bigger issue is the safety net. If the bubble bursts, central banks
can't come to the rescue as aggressively as in 2001 while inﬂation
is above target. Fiscal space is also far more limited than a quarter
of a century ago.
But don't rule out a diﬀerent kind of rescue. AI is not just an
economic tool; it is also about power – geopolitical and
technological. If things went south for the sector, some form of

government support would be conceivable, from public

### Page 19

procurement to guarantees or targeted support for strategically
important companies. The dotcom bust was left to the market. An
AI bust might not be.
Monetary policy is unlikely to pop this bubble. But it could decide
how hard the landing is – and Washington may decide who gets a
parachute.
Bubble or not?
What do valuations and revenue gaps tell
us?
Bubble-like pockets, but not a bubble across the board: the leaders
are hugely proﬁtable, while the real uncertainty sits with the AI

labs and the revenue gap.
Valuations ﬁrst. At its 2000 peak, Cisco traded at well over 100
times earnings. Today’s AI leaders are expensive, but proﬁtable,
and analysts expect earnings growth to bring their multiples down
considerably. Microsoft’s P/E is expected to fall from 29.2x based on
ﬁscal 2026 earnings to 21.9x in 2028, Amazon’s from 28x based on
2025 earnings to 21x in 2027, and Alphabet’s from 20.9x to 17.9x
over the same period. With earnings estimates being higher than a

couple of months ago, that does not look particularly stretched.
The larger uncertainty sits with the AI labs, whose valuations follow
the venture capital playbook: spectacular revenue growth,
uncertain long-term economics.
Then the revenue gap. Capex is running ahead of AI revenues, as it
can take several years for investment to translate fully into
revenues. Amazon, Microsoft and Alphabet have invested around
US$600bn over 2023-25. As a rough rule of thumb, every dollar of

AI infrastructure may need to generate 40-50 cents of annual
revenue (assuming hyperscaler-like margins), implying US$240-
300bn a year – although not all of this capex was AI-related. That
looks large, but less so next to labour costs. At US$100,000 per
employee, US$300bn is the cost of three million workers. AI doesn't

### Page 20

need to replace them: a 20% productivity gain across 15 million
employees would deliver the same value. Consumers alone won't
close the gap; the money has to come from enterprises.
Two caveats. Concentration: the weight of the largest tech stocks in
the S&P 500 exceeds the dotcom peak, so any disappointment
would hit the whole index hard. And circularity in ﬁnancing and
revenues makes true end-demand harder to measure.
Finally, a bubble in prices doesn't mean the technology is a dud.

Railways had a bubble. So did the internet. Both still changed the
world.
What could end the boom prematurely:
disappointing demand, power bottlenecks, a
cheaper-model breakthrough, or Taiwan?
Most likely not a single trigger, but a slow squeeze: overcapacity,
eroding margins and tighter ﬁnancing if the revenue gap fails to
close.
Start with the usual suspects. Demand could disappoint if
enterprise adoption stalls and pilots fail to turn into proﬁts. Power

bottlenecks are more likely to cap the pace of the boom than to end
it, and could even stretch it out. A Taiwan crisis remains a low-
probability, extreme-impact event.
Cheaper models are the most intriguing risk. The DeepSeek shock of
January 2025 wiped almost US$600bn oﬀ Nvidia's market value in
a single day. Cheaper compute can raise demand – the Jevons
paradox – but higher volumes can come with lower margins. That
makes the proﬁtability of AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic the

big question mark. If it disappoints, the hardware will still be
needed, but the build-out would likely slow. Nvidia's exceptionally
high margins could also normalise if competition increases or the
scarcity value of leading-edge compute declines.
Missing from most lists is ﬁnancing: rising bond yields, a credit
event at a leveraged neocloud or private credit fund, or an energy
shock that keeps central banks hawkish.

### Page 21

Ultimately, we see the biggest risk in overcapacity. Continued
innovation in chips, more eﬃcient models and optimised
algorithms could eventually create an imbalance between supply
and demand, pushing down utilisation and returns. For now,
though, the industry is still struggling to scale compute and secure
enough power, while new business models and applications keep
emerging.
There is one more way to read today's capacity commitments: as
strategic entry deterrence. Announcing gigantic investment plans

discourages competitors from entering the market – and some of
those plans may never be fully executed if returns disappoint. That
would be a quieter ending than a crash. Not with a bang, but with a
series of downward revisions to capex guidance.
If tech stocks fall 30%, how much does the
wealth effect hit US consumption?
Less than you might think, but more than during the dotcom bust –
and the real damage would come through investment and jobs.
US equity wealth is highly concentrated. The top 20% of

households by income (earning more than $155k a year) hold
84.1% of equities, mutual funds and deﬁned-contribution pension
assets. That limits the wealth eﬀect. Fed research suggests the
marginal propensity to consume out of wealth fell from around 3.5
cents per dollar (1964-2011) to 2.6 cents in 2020.
A 30% stock market correction would wipe out roughly $24.5tn of
household wealth. At 2.6 cents per dollar, that means a drop in
consumer spending of around $640bn, or 2.9% of nominal

consumer spending (an annualised $22.3tn in August). With wealth
even more concentrated than in 2020, the multiplier is probably
weaker; at 2.2 cents, the hit would be around 2.4%. That is a level
eﬀect, typically playing out over one to two years – but a big
number nonetheless.
History oﬀers some comfort: during the dotcom bust, US consumer
spending barely ﬂinched. But it could be worse this time. First,
wealthy households are currently the only ones holding up

### Page 22

spending; the bottom 60%, who spend roughly as many dollars as
the top 20%, are squeezed by tariﬀs and higher fuel prices. Second,
the Fed has less room to cut than in 2001. Third, the credit cushion
is thinner: rising delinquencies on car loans and credit cards show
stress building at lower incomes.
The good news: this is not 2008. US household debt stands at
around 70% of GDP, the same as in 2001 and well below the 100%
reached in 2007.
In the end, the wealth eﬀect is the smaller channel. After the

dotcom bust, business investment fell by 11% in two years. IT
equipment was the least of it, falling just 6%, transport equipment
fell 21% and manufacturing buildings plunged by a whopping
55%. Tech may light the fuse, but the explosion happens elsewhere.
What would a tech sell-off mean for the
dollar and FX markets?
Not necessarily the classic safe-haven dollar rally – this time, a
tech sell-oﬀ could well hurt the dollar.
The usual playbook says risk-oﬀ means dollar up. But an AI-led

correction would be a US-centric shock. Foreign investors hold large,
partly unhedged positions in US equities, built up over a decade of
American outperformance. A sell-oﬀ in US tech could trigger
repatriation and higher hedge ratios, both dollar-negative – as in
the 'sell America' episode of April 2025. The IMF estimates the
dollar would fall by around 6% in an AI-led equity correction.
The rates channel points in the same direction. Negative wealth

eﬀects, weaker consumption and slower US growth would
eventually push the Fed towards rate cuts, narrowing interest rate
diﬀerentials against the dollar. The timing would depend on
inﬂation: with prices still running above target, the Fed may cut
later than markets hope, which could limit the dollar's fall in the
early stages.
Among the winners, the yen and the Swiss franc would play their
traditional safe-haven role. The euro could beneﬁt relatively, given

Europe's lower exposure to both AI capex and equity wealth (4Q). A

### Page 23

tech crash made in America would, for once, be a reason to hold
European assets rather than ﬂee them.
The losers would be the currencies of Asian tech exporters, notably
the Taiwan dollar and the Korean won, whose economies are
leveraged bets on AI demand. They would come under heavy
pressure, although intervention by local authorities and the legacy
of huge trade surpluses would cushion the blow somewhat.
The bottom line: in an AI bust, the dollar may lose its reputation as

the place to hide. Not because the world has found a better safe
haven, but because this time the ﬁre would start in the safe house.
Our base case for 2027: boom, plateau or
bust, and which signposts should readers
watch?
Our base case is a plateau at altitude: the boom continues in 2027,
but its contribution to growth peaks. A bust remains the main risk
scenario.
Early indicators point to tight semiconductor supply into 2028,
suggesting the investment cycle still has legs. But as capex growth

slows, AI's contribution to US growth should fade.
The diﬀerence between the two scenarios matters enormously for
the US. A plateau would give the non-tech economy room to
breathe: capital, energy and workers no longer absorbed by AI
could ﬂow back, allowing non-tech investment – and potentially
employment – to rebound. A bust would be a very diﬀerent animal:
an abrupt choking oﬀ of tech investment, similar to 2001,
combined with a negative wealth eﬀect on consumers through

falling stock markets. And this time, central banks would have less
room for rate cuts as inﬂation remains their main concern.
Which signposts should readers watch? For the boom itself, the key
indicators are hyperscaler capex guidance, Taiwanese order books
and lead times, and AI revenues relative to capex. Markets will focus
on realised AI revenues at the large technology ﬁrms, margins on
incremental revenue and how the next phase of the build-out will

On ﬁnancing, credit spreads on AI-related debt, private credit ﬂows

and redemptions, and bond yields and term premia will show
whether investors remain willing to fund the build-out. In the real
economy, the questions are whether non-tech investment ﬁnally
recovers, how hiring in AI-exposed sectors develops, and what
productivity, grid connection and power data are telling us.
The question for 2027 isn't whether AI will change the world. It's
whether anyone gets paid for it in time. If earnings fail to match
sky-high expectations, the plateau could turn out to be a ledge.

### Page 2

The global AI trade should make a nice comeback now that the latest
issue of The Economist sees the bottom falling out of it:
The weekly financial magazine, famous for its contrarian cover stories,
also recently picked the top in global bond yields when it ran a cover story
titled "Will bonds blow up?" in the October 10-16 issue. Then again, bond
yields rose worldwide last week. Central bankers sounded more hawkish,
and inflation remains sticky. Bond investors expect more monetary policy

tightening ahead.

### Page 3

Stock markets barely moved last week. Global profits are booming, yet
valuations keep falling, almost everywhere. We think they are at or near a
bottom. Gold continues to falter, and China’s problems are depressing
stock prices there. Here’s more:
I. Stock Market Derby
The US has held its ground in November. The US MSCI’s share of the All
Country World MSCI’s market capitalization is 65.3% (chart). The Europe
MSCI’s share is 13.5%. The Emerging Markets (EM) MSCI’s share has

slipped to 11.0%.
The US MSCI’s share of global forward earnings hasn’t kept pace. It has
edged down to 54.1% from 54.3% two weeks ago, well below its market-
cap share (chart). The EM MSCI’s earnings share has climbed to 20.6%
even as its market-cap share has fallen. Investors are pricing the South
Korea and Taiwan MSCIs at historically low multiples.

### Page 4

The Eurozone and UK ETFs have gained on the US ETF in recent months
(chart). The All Country World ex-US ETF continues to track the US ETF
closely. The US has pulled back less from its recent high.
Global stock markets have gone mostly sideways this month (chart). The
All Country World ETF is flat mtd. The US is in the middle of the pack,
down 0.2%. Turkey leads with a 9.0% gain. South Korea (-6.6%) and
Taiwan (-3.7%) remain at the bottom of the performance list.

### Page 5

II. Stock Market Earnings & Valuation
US and overseas forward earnings continue to climb together to record
highs (chart). The two have tracked each other closely since 2024.
Global profits are booming. The US MSCI’s forward profit margin has risen
to a record high of 16.9% (chart). The Canada MSCI’s is even higher at
17.1%. The EM MSCI’s forward profit margin has jumped to 13.9%. And
the All Country World ex-US MSCI’s is up to 12.9%.

### Page 6

Valuations have been falling everywhere. The US MSCI’s forward P/E is
down to 18.5 (chart). The EMU MSCI’s is 14.3, the Japan MSCI’s is 14.1,
and the UK MSCI’s is 12.0. The EM MSCI trades at a forward P/E of just
8.4. We think global valuations are at or near bottoming. Further downside
looks limited to us.
III. Bonds
Bond yields rose this week on hawkish central bank commentary and
stubborn inflation (chart). The 10-year US Treasury yield jumped to 5.16%

from 4.86% a week earlier. The UK’s 10-year government bond yield of
5.34% remains the highest in the group.

### Page 7

The spread between French and German 10-year yields widened to
154.2bps (chart). That’s below its October high. France’s increase this
week was much bigger than those of other European countries.
Two-year government bond yields are well above central bank policy rates
(chart). These yields are pricing in continued global tightening for the
foreseeable future.

### Page 8

IV. Gold
Gold’s latest correction resembles its pullbacks in the mid-1970s and
during the Great Financial Crisis (chart). Those corrections occurred
during or right after recessions.
Gold has had a disappointing year so far. The nearby futures price broke
below support at $4,000 per ounce and is now $3,879.70 (chart). That's
well below its 200-day moving average. We don’t see what might lift the
gold price in the near term. We are pushing back our $5,000 target to the

end of 2027. The main bullish driver is central bank gold buying. The main
bearish factor is rising interest rates.

### Page 9

V. China
The China MSCI stock price index has been falling since late last year
(chart). Forward earnings has been rising slowly, while the forward P/E
has declined more quickly to about 10.0.
China’s property bust keeps getting worse. The Shenzhen Real Estate
Stock Price Index has fallen to 897.61, further below the 1,000 support
that had held since 2012 (chart). On October 18, we wrote that we would
worry if it broke below that level. We remain concerned. Exports continue

to carry the economy, while domestic consumption remains very weak.

### Page 10

Chinese tech stocks took another hit last week. The China Technology
ETF (CQQQ) fell 4.4% (chart). Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned on
November 12 amid reports linking him to chip shipments to China. Alibaba
stock fell 14%, and Baidu stock fell 4.6% last week as the news hit.
Investors fear Washington will further tighten restrictions on China’s
access to Nvidia’s processors. We continue to favor the EM ex-China
MSCI over the China MSCI.
💡

log in to the Yardeni QuickTakes website and post them at the end
of the QuickTakes article. Paid members' contributions may be
featured in our segment, "Ed Answers Your Questions".

### Page 11

Document 379: National Bank of Hungary review_ The doves aren’t gone yet

Source PDF: all report pdf/National Bank of Hungary review_ The doves aren’t gone yet.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

The National Bank of Hungary kept rates unchanged in
November, but delivered a dovish twist. A rate cut was already
on the table, and improving domestic risk sentiment is creating
more room for manoeuvre. Nevertheless, we only see the next
cuts coming in late 2027 as inﬂation pressures rebuild

Peter Virovacz and Zoltán Homolya
The National Bank of Hungary, Budapest
5.50% Key interest rate
(no change)
As expected
ING's policy view: A dovish twist, but no
rush

### Page 2

The National Bank of Hungary kept the base rate unchanged at
5.50% on 17 November, in line with widespread expectations. This
marked the third consecutive meeting with rates being kept on
hold. While the latest communication revealed somewhat more
room for monetary easing, we think the window of opportunity will
remain narrow.
Looking ahead, the exceptionally favourable domestic inﬂation
backdrop is likely to fade relatively quickly. We see inﬂation

accelerating to 3.0% by December 2026, with repricing at the start
of next year temporarily pushing price pressures closer to 3.5% in
the ﬁrst quarter of 2027. Inﬂation could then approach 4% during
the summer, largely due to base eﬀects. In our view, this
increasingly challenging inﬂation proﬁle means that any near-term
opportunity for easing may prove short-lived.
In the meantime, however, we see the latest dovish shift in
communication potentially testing the waters and gauging market

sensitivity to eventual easing, particularly in a global environment
where hawkishness still dominates. The key litmus test will be the
forint: a muted FX reaction to the dovish rhetoric would give the
central bank greater conﬁdence that some easing could be
delivered without jeopardising market stability. This means that a
possible one-and-done December rate cut has become a clear
dovish risk to our baseline.
However, we believe that any near-term window for easing would

be relatively narrow. As 2027 progresses, the main constraint on
monetary policy is likely to shift gradually from external risks
towards the domestic inﬂation outlook. With inﬂation expected to
move closer to 4% during the summer, we see little room for a
sustained easing cycle over the next six to nine months. Any near-
term easing would therefore be more likely to represent an
opportunistic move than the beginning of a conventional cutting
cycle.

A more durable easing window could open later in 2027, once
inﬂation starts to decelerate more convincingly after the summer. If
this coincides with a benign external environment and a further

### Page 3

decline in the risk premium on Hungarian assets, we see room for
one or two rate cuts in the fourth quarter of 2027. Our baseline
therefore sees the base rate reaching 5.00% by end-2027, followed
by a further reduction to 4.50% by end-2028.
Our three takeaways from the presser

### Page 4

Review Policy rate NBH National Bank of Hungary
Monetary policy Hungary
1. A cut was already on the table. The biggest dovish surprise
came during the press conference. The Monetary Council
considered two options: keeping rates unchanged or cutting
them, while the decision itself was not unanimous. This
suggests that the bar for easing may already be lower than
previously thought. The December Inﬂation Report will now be
particularly important, with Governor Mihály Varga explicitly

stating that favourable developments in Hungary's risk
assessment and inﬂation outlook, together with reduced
external uncertainty, could increase its room for manoeuvre.
2. Hungary's risk story has improved. Perhaps the most notable
change in the narrative is the assessment of Hungary's risk
premium. Varga highlighted the favourable market reception of
the government's ﬁscal plans and its commitment to euro
adoption as factors supporting Hungary's risk assessment and

increasing room for monetary policy. The forint has also
remained stable and is stronger than a year ago, helping to
contain inﬂationary pressures. In other words, domestic
ﬁnancial market developments are becoming less of a
constraint on monetary policy than the Monetary Council
previously considered them to be.
3. The external environment still demands caution. The greater
room for manoeuvre does not mean that the NBH is ready to
embark on an easing cycle. Energy price volatility, elevated

global yields and above-target inﬂation in major economies
continue to warrant caution. At home, inﬂation is also expected
to accelerate from its current 2% level due to fuel prices, tax
changes and other policy measures. This leaves the Monetary
Council in a familiar data-dependent position: its room for
manoeuvre has increased, but whether it can actually use that
room will depend on both the external environment and the
December inﬂation outlook.

Document 413: Three Questions About China’s Hidden Foreign Exchange Assets (2026-11-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Three Questions About China’s Hidden Foreign Exchange Assets (2026-11-12).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 17
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
Three Questions About China’s Hidden Foreign
Exchange Assets
A trillion-dollar gap in foreign exchange purchases raises questions about where
China’s foreign currency assets are held.
Carlos Rawlins/Reuters
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
PUBLISHED
November 11, 2026 8:32 p.m.
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
The PBOC argues—unpersuasively, I think—that it
doesn’t have any hidden foreign exchange reserves. I
would reframe that as hidden foreign currency assets,
as foreign currency lent out (or swapped out) with
domestic financial institutions is a classic way of
masking reserves.
Many in Asia—and some in the IMF—are inclined
to believe the PBOC. Fair enough, it is hard to

challenge an institution as large and powerful (in
some ways) as the PBOC.

### Page 3

3
But to convince, the PBOC really needs to provide
answers to three questions.
1. What explains the current gap between foreign
exchange settlement and the PBOC’s reserves?
Settlement is the purchase of foreign exchange in the
market by the PBOC and the state banks. It
technically implies taking an open foreign currency
position, as the foreign exchange is being bought with
renminbi.
Historically, it correlated with changes in the PBOC’s
balance sheet.

And some gaps before 2012 can be explained. In late
2005 and 2006, the PBOC did over $100 billion in
swaps with the state banks, who used the FX to buy
foreign bonds (portfolio debt outflows surged). Those

### Page 4

4
swaps were unwound after the global financial crisis,
and actually provided some of the foreign currency
that “funded” the China Investment Corporation
when it initially scaled up.
And in 2007 and 2008, the PBOC required that the
state banks hold FX against their domestic RMB
deposits (to keep the FX out of reserves; it was held
on deposit at the PBOC and shows up as “other
foreign assets” on the PBOC’s balance sheet and as
“other, other, short-term” in the balance of payments.*

After 2018, though, there are unexplained gaps
between the FX bought in settlement and the
PBOC’s balance sheet (technically cumulative
monthly changes to capture the fact that FX is held as
its initial CNY purchase price).
There was a net accumulation of FX outside the
PBOC’s balance sheet in 2020 and 2021, when the
CNY was under pressure to appreciate. There are
modest sales in 2023 and 2024 when there is
depreciation pressure. And there are large purchases

in 2025 and the first 9 months of 2026—total
purchases in settlement in the last 12 months (spot)
are $500 to 600 billion, while the increase in the
PBOC’s balance sheet is only around $50 billion over
the same period.

### Page 5

5
The gap over time is now $1 trillion. The PBOC
needs to be able to answer a simple question: who
holds this FX, and who holds the associated FX
market risk (given the FX has been purchased with
RMB).
The monthly pattern of growth in settlement strongly
suggests it is backdoor intervention; there are sales
when spot is weak (economically) relative to the fix,
and purchases when spot is either at the fix or just
stronger than the fix (the strong edge of the band

hasn’t been used). The pattern of purchases and sales
thus looks exactly like that of a central bank
maintaining a tight band around a (crawling) peg.
If the PBOC cannot credibly explain this gap, there
isn’t much more to discuss. The PBOC argues that it
hasn’t swapped FX with the state banks or put foreign
currency on deposit at the state banks. Fair enough.

### Page 6

6
But someone is buying in settlement; the PBOC
ought to know who…
2. Missing interest income
There is an obvious tell here—Bloomberg reported
back in 2022 that the PBOC transferred $158 billion
in accumulated interest income to the Ministry of
Finance as part of a special dividend to hold the
budget deficit down. Again, fair enough. But
absolutely nothing changed in the FX reserves held
on the PBOC’s balance sheet or in the balance of
payments.
That highlights a broader issue. Since the end of

2016, the PBOC’s balance sheet FX hasn’t really
changed.

### Page 7

7
Peer institutions generally have an upward slope to
their reserves as a result of “gains from reserve
management” or “reinvestment of interest income.” **
The Central Bank of China (Taipei) and Japan’s
Ministry of Finance are good examples.
The interest on ~$3 trillion in reserves should be
around $100 billion a year. A bit less during the “low-
for-long” era before the pandemic, when SAFE wasn’t
getting much income out of its euro portfolio. A bit

more now, when U.S. short-term rates are well above 3
percent and SAFE’s legacy agencies carry yields of 5
or 6 percent. $100 billion over 10 years is real money
—roughly a trillion dollars.
If SAFE has steadily been selling its interest income,
it should say so (the fact that BOP reserves have often
diverged from the PBOC balance sheet suggests a
more complex story).

### Page 8

8
Moreover, if SAFE has been transferring the FX to
one of China’s many sovereign wealth funds
(including those controlled by SAFE) or the policy
banks, it should say so.
But SAFE cannot say what you see is what we really
have if it cannot explain how it handles the interest
on its now $3.5 trillion in reserves.
3. The accounting for the PBOC’s entrusted loans
and other foreign currency claims on domestic
financial institutions, notably the policy banks

FX handed over to a sovereign wealth fund to manage
doesn’t appear in reserves. It isn’t a liquid reserve asset.
But it should appear elsewhere in the balance of
payments as either portfolio debt or portfolio equity.
And FX handed over to the policy banks through
mechanisms such as entrusted loans (or a simple
deposit or swap) doesn’t count as a foreign reserve—
the foreign currency claim is on a domestic financial
institution, and that isn’t considered a “foreign” asset.

There is no doubt that the PBOC has domestic
claims on China’s financial institutions. The use of
“entrusted” loans to fund the policy banks in 2010
was reported by Caixin and the Financial Times.

### Page 9

9
$95 billion in entrusted loans were converted into
equity in the CDB and China Exim back in 2015
(giving SAFE majority equity stakes in both
institutions through its Buttonwood investment
vehicle).
And the SAFE’s 2020 annual report contained a ten-
year progress report on the “diversified use” of China’s
FX (basically, the non-reserve use) which indicated
that SAFE continued to provide entrusted loans to
small, medium, and large Chinese financial

institutions.
Now perhaps these instruments have been unwound
—the China Development Corporation (per Stephen
Green, a friend) has reduced its foreign currency
balance sheet over the last few years. But that should
lead the foreign currency to flow back to SAFE
absent another use—and that increase in FX isn’t
showing up in the PBOC’s reserves.
In other words, SAFE should provide a transparent
accounting of the FX that has been bought but
transferred to other financial institutions and any

ongoing foreign currency exposure to the domestic
banking system (including the policy banks).
These aren’t big asks. The settlement gap is big and
obvious, interest income is a known issue, and SAFE
itself has reported its use of entrusted loans in the
past. There is no issue around the past use of swaps

### Page 10

10
either. A substantial share of the banks’ foreign
currency assets are funded domestically. It should be
possible for SAFE to explain the role, if any, it has
played in this funding.
There is, of course, another issue around China’s
balance of payments: the growth in foreign currency
external assets funded out of domestic foreign
currency deposits.
China argues that the growing foreign assets of its
state banks are simply a natural part of a growing

domestic financial system. But there is more to the
story.
Onshore FX deposits have tended to rise when
economics say they should not, and fall when the
economics say they should (offshore FX deposits
behave as expected). In 2020 and 2021, FX deposits

### Page 11

11
rose even when there was more yield on RMB
deposits.
And RMB deposits have continued to rise in 2025
and 2026 even with the RMB’s appreciation
offsetting the higher dollar deposit rate. This isn’t an
accident: banks just raised the interest rate that they
pay on dollar deposits to encourage exporters NOT to
convert their export proceeds into RMB and thus
take pressure off the PBOC (per Reuters).
In other words, the pattern of the banks’ foreign

currency asset growth suggests that they are offsetting
appreciation pressure by bidding for FX (or receiving
it from other state actors), not that FX flows into the
banks during periods when onshore residents worry
about depreciation.

### Page 12

12
Right now, the “settlement gap” is the most pressing
issue. FX deposit growth in excess of FX loan growth
can explain FX outflows of around $150 billion in the
four quarters to Q3. The FX outflows implied by
settlement reached $600 billion over the last four
quarters. Together they account for the bulk of
China’s reported $825 billion plus (I estimate a Q4
2025 to Q3 2026 surplus of around $900 billion) in
outflows implied by the reported current account.***

### Page 13

13
It goes without saying that this matters because the
weak RMB is the central reason for China’s massive
export outperformance since 2023—and that export
outperformance has continued in 2026.****
* I have been doing this a long time; I actually do
know most of the historical quirks in China’s balance

### Page 14

14
of payments data.
** The MoF can count a portion of its unrepatriated
gains from accumulated interest on its large reserves
against the budget deficit, but since the actual
interest is held abroad, it still needs to borrow
domestically against those reserves (domestic JGB
issuance and foreign reserves both go up).
*** My estimate of a larger “true” current account
comes from moving gold imports over to the financial
account, and from an assumption that undercounting

of the reinvested earnings of funds held offshore
explains the obvious problem in the investment
income account.
**** The spike in imports in Q1 wasn’t entirely real.
China was slow to adjust its import price index for
the rise in semiconductor/chip prices. This inflated
both import and export volumes in Q1. The rise in
gold imports this year also registers in the import
volume series—though the rise is best understood as
“importing” a financial asset.

### Page 15

15

zig-zag fashion, with a crisis-aversion relief rally on Wednesday, a plunge
on Thursday as the thought of AI data center roadblocks chilled investor
optimism, and a bit of a recovery Friday as leveler heads prevailed (speed
bumps yes, roadblocks no). … Also: The AI race faces three intensifying
constraints—financial, political, and physical, the first two becoming acute.

Document 506: 不動産市場で存在感を増す私募ファンド ～多様な運用戦略とREITとの補完関係～

Flash Insights

2026 年 11 月 6 日

1/4 
不動産市場で存在感を増す私募ファンド 
～多様な運用戦略と REIT との補完関係～ 
 資産運用調査部 シニア研究員  河谷 善夫

1．大きく残高を増やしてきた私募ファンド 
資料 1 は、我が国の不動産証券化の主体別保有資産額の推移と、2015 年度から 2025 年度までの
年平均増加率（CAGR）を示したものである。

資料 1 不動産証券化の主体別保有資産額の推移（左）と年平均増加率（CAGR）（右） 
<保有資産額の推移>    <年平均増加率（CAGR）>

（注 1）J-REIT、私募リート、不動産特定共同事業は各年度末、私募ファンドは各年度の 12 月末時点。年平均増加率は、それぞ
れの系列の 2015 年度と 2025 年度の比較値から算出。なお、資産額は、保有する不動産・信託受益権の残高を示し、年間の
取得額や投資家の出資額ではない。 
（注 2）不動産特定共同事業とは、不動産特定共同事業法に基づき、国土交通大臣や都道府県知事などの許可・登録を受けて
行われるもの。複数の投資家から出資金を募り、不動産を売買・賃貸・運用して得られた利益を分配する事業。不動産クラウド
ファンディングの多くが活用し、少額からの投資が可能となっている。 
（注 3）不動産私募ファンドには、広義には私募リートも含まれる。私募リートは、投信法に基づく非上場の投資法人である。運用
期間を定めず、一定の条件の下で投資口の払戻しに応じる（オープンエンド）。一方、それ以外の私募ファンドには、合同会社
や特定目的会社等を用い、運用期間を定め、投資家の請求による期中の払戻しを行わないものが多い。本稿では、私募リート
を除く不動産私募ファンドを「私募ファンド」と呼ぶ。なお、私募リート以外の私募ファンドにも、無期限・オープンエンド型の商品
がある。 
（出所）国土交通省「不動産証券化の実態調査」及びARES J-REIT Databook（2026 年）を基に第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作
成

2015 年度時点の比較では、J-REITの保有資産額が最も大きかったが、2020 年代に入り、私募フ
ァンド、私募リートの保有資産額は、J-REITを上回るペースで増加している。 
2025 年度時点でみると、私募ファンドの保有資産額は約39.5 兆円（2025 年 12 月末）と、J-

### Page 2

2/4 
REITの約24.5 兆円（2026 年3 月末）を約 15 兆円上回っている。この間の年平均増加率で最も大
きいのは、私募リートだが、私募ファンドも年11％を超える伸びを示した。 
もっとも、保有資産には借入金を活用して取得したものも含まれるため、その残高増加を、投
資家からの新規出資額の増加と同一視することはできない。また、残高の増加だけで運用成績を
判断することもできない。一方、不動産の保有・運用を担う主体として、私募ファンドの存在感
が増していることは確認できる。近年の拡大ペースの違いを考えるうえでは、資金調達経路の違
いも重要である。J-REITでは、投資口価格の低迷が公募増資を通じた物件取得の制約となり得
る。実際、2025 年は、投資口価格が 1 口当たりの純資産価値（NAV）を下回る銘柄が多く、公募増
資が低調だった。一方、私募ファンドは、投資家の意向に応じた投資対象・運用戦略を設定して
出資を募る仕組みであり、上場市場とは異なる経路で不動産投資資金を受け入れている。こうし
た違いが、近年の資産拡大ペースの差に関係している可能性がある。 
本稿では、私募ファンドの仕組みや運用戦略を整理し、REITとの役割の違いを踏まえて、今後
の注目点を示す。

2．私募ファンドの仕組みと資金の出し手 
私募ファンドは、特定の投資家から集めた出資金と金融機関からの借入金等を用いて、不動産
を取得・運用する仕組みである。代表的な形態には、合同会社が匿名組合出資を受けるGK-TKスキ
ーム（資料2 左）や、特定目的会社を用いるTMKスキーム（資料2 右）がある。運用会社（アセッ
トマネジメント会社）が投資対象や運用方針に応じて物件の取得・管理・売却等を行う。

資料 2 私募ファンドの仕組みの概要 
<GK-TKスキームの例>    <TMKスキームの例>

（注）匿名組合出資は、合同会社が行う事業に出資し、その利益の分配を受ける契約に基づく出資。優先出資は、TMKの特定出
資に対し、利益配当又は残余財産の分配で優先する出資であり、借入金・社債より優先するものではない。特定出資等は図示を
省略。 
（出所）第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

GK-TKスキームでは、合同会社が投資家からの匿名組合出資と借入金により、通常、不動産信託
受益権を取得する。不動産自体は信託銀行等が所有し、合同会社はその収益等を受け取る。一
方、TMKスキームでは、特定目的会社が優先出資や借入金・社債等によって資金を調達し、不動産
を直接所有するか、不動産信託受益権を保有する。

### Page 3

3/4 
私募ファンドは、こうした法的な仕組みだけでなく、運用戦略や投資目的も多様である。資料 3
では、その代表例を整理する。

資料 3 私募ファンドの主な運用戦略・目的

（注）代表例を示したものであり、各区分は必ずしも相互に排他的ではない。 
（出所）第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

私募ファンドに出資する投資家としては、国内外の年金基金、保険会社、金融機関、政府系フ
ァンド、事業会社等がある。 
こうした資金を受け入れるファンドの運用戦略は、資料 3 に示したように、安定した賃料収入
を重視するものから、開発等による価値向上を目指すものまで幅広い。このように私募ファンド
は、単にJ-REITより流動性が低い投資商品というだけでなく、投資対象、保有期間、開発・改修
の有無等に応じた多様な投資機会を提供するものといえる。 
流動性の低さは投資上の制約であるが、長期資金を持つ投資家にとっては、それだけで投資対
象から外れるわけではない。投資対象や運用戦略を選択し、専門的な運用を通じて収益を獲得す
る手段として、私募ファンドが活用されている。

3．REITとの役割の違いと今後の注目点 
J-REITや私募リートが主として不動産の継続的な保有・運用による賃料収入を重視するのに対
し、私募ファンドには、開発や改修による価値向上と、その後の売却を組み合わせるものもあ
る。長期保有型も含む多様な戦略の中で、開発・価値向上段階のリスクを引き受ける資金の受け
皿となっている点に、私募ファンドの役割の一つがある。 
私募ファンドのうち、あらかじめ運用期間を定める有期ファンドでは、物件売却による資金回
収を予定する場合、その時期も運用計画に組み込まれる。契約等に基づいて運用期間を延長でき
る場合もあるが、延長の可否・条件や、必要に応じた借換えへの対応を考慮する必要がある。こ
のため、運用期限を設けないJ-REITや私募リートとは異なり、想定した時期・価格で売却できる
かに加え、売却を延期する場合の条件にも注意が必要である。 
また、私募ファンドの中には、将来のREIT等への売却を想定して物件を一時的に保有する「ブ
リッジファンド」もある。REITへの売却を想定するこうしたファンドでは、売却先となるREITの
取得余力も重要となる。このため、私募ファンドの拡大を、REITに代わる投資先の成長とのみ捉
えるのは十分ではない。開発や価値向上を担い、REIT等の長期保有主体に物件を供給する補完的

### Page 4

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
4/4 
な役割にも目を向ける必要がある。 
今後は、私募ファンドの資金調達環境に加え、運用戦略の実行可能性や売却先となるREIT等の
取得余力にも着目し、市場拡大の持続性を見極めていくことが重要である。

Document 523: 中央銀行と闘うな 利上げ局面で株式は弱い

Source PDF: all report pdf/中央銀行と闘うな 利上げ局面で株式は弱い.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

2026年 11月 17日(火)

1/4 
中央銀行と闘うな 利上げ局面で株式は弱い    
 経済調査部主席エコノミスト藤代宏一 
（℡：050-5474-6123）

・最近のＡＩ・半導体株を中心とする世界的な株価調整は、米中間選挙における民主党の躍進
に伴うＡＩ投資の増勢鈍化懸念、エヌビディアＣＥＯのスキャンダルおよび同氏の辞任に絡
んだ「商慣行」の見直し、そして対中半導体規制強化（半導体へのリモートアクセスを禁止
する動き）のほか、ごく直近ではアンソロピック社の大型ＩＰＯに伴う換金売りなどが背景
にある。何れもＡＩ・半導体関連株に対して逆風の材料である。

・そうしたなか、世界的にＡＩ・半導体銘柄のＰＥＲは顕著に低下している。ＰＥＲ低下の数
値的な背景は、予想ＥＰＳが切り上がるなかで株価が下落しているからである。S&P500にお
ける「情報技術セクター」や、世界の代表的な半導体株で構成されるＳＯＸ指数などのＰＥ
Ｒが注目されている。こうしたＰＥＲ低下を割安感の台頭（割高感の解消）と読み好感すべ
きか、それともＡＩ投資の減速を織り込んだ業績悪化懸念の広がりと読むべきか、これが目
下の最大の関心事である。

・日経平均株価は先行き 12ヶ月 72,000円程度で推移するだろう 
・USD/JPYは先行き 12ヶ月 155円程度で推移するだろう 
・日銀は政策金利を 12月に 1.5％とした後、27年末までに 2.25％とするだろう 
・FEDはFF金利（誘導目標上限）を、12月に4.25％へ引き上げるだろう

### Page 2

2/4

・ＰＥＲ低下はＡＩの将来に対する文脈で語られがちだが、別の角度から説明することもでき
る。あまりにも単純過ぎると𠮟られてしまいそうだが、それはＦｅｄを中心とする主要中銀
の利上げである。奇しくも日米欧中銀は９月に同時利上げを実施し、12月にも再度同時利上
げとなる公算が現時点では大きい。日銀も10月展望レポートで「世界的にインフレ圧力が高
まるなか、夏場以降、米欧を含む多くの中央銀行は利上げ方向に政策を転換しており、こう
したグローバルな金融環境の変化が、国際金融市場等を通じてわが国の経済・物価に及ぼす
影響には留意が必要である」と記述していた。2024～2025年は日銀が利上げを続けていたの
をよそに、欧米中銀が利下げ方向に動いていたため、世界全体でみれば金融環境は緩和方向
にあった。それに対して2026年後半以降、主要中銀は同時に金融引き締め方向に動いてお
り、世界全体として株式市場にストレスがかかりやすい状況にある。金利上昇による資金調
達コストの増加がＡＩ投資そのものを減速させ得る経路もあれば、安全資産である国債利回
りの上昇が株式の相対的な魅力を低下させ株価を下押しするという経路もある。

・ここで米10年実質金利とS&P500やNASDAQの予想ＰＥＲの推移を確認すると、この間のＰＥＲ
低下（≒株価下落）は実質金利の上昇に沿っていることがわかる。実質金利は５月に２％を
超えた後、Ｆｅｄの利上げ観測の高まりに伴って現在は2.9％近傍まで水準を切り上げてお
り、これが株式のバリュエーション（ＰＥＲ）を下押ししている可能性が高い。Ｆｅｄの金
融引き締めがＰＥＲ低下を招いた例としては2018年と2022年があり、今回も利上げが株価へ
打撃を与えるという単純な結果になりつつある。「中央銀行と闘うな」という格言が思い出
される。

### Page 3

3/4

・2018年後半の株価下落はＦｅｄの利上げ進行中に発生した。2018年10～12月の株価急落（９
月20日の高値から19.8％下落）は、米中貿易戦争の激化によって米国経済に対する不安が強
まっていたにもかかわらず、Ｆｅｄが利上げを継続するとの懸念が支配的になる局面であっ
た。米国債市場においては長短金利差が縮小し、一部の年限（５年金利と２年金利）では逆
イールドが発生するなど、市場参加者が景気減速を織り込むのをよそに、Ｆｅｄは2018年３
月、６月、９月と利上げを続け、株価の下落基調が強まっていた12月にも断行し、政策金利
（ＦＦ金利誘導目標上限）を2.50％に引き上げた。転機となったのは、2019年１月４日のパ
ウエル議長の発言。株式市場をなだめる意図もあってか「政策判断を忍耐強く行い、市場の
示すリスクに注意を払い、必要なら政策姿勢を大きく変更する」と軌道修正を図った。結
局、株価は2018年12月24日が大底となり、その後はＦｅｄの利下げ（2019年7・9・10月）を
追い風として、コロナ禍に直面するまでS&P500は10％超の下落を経験することなく上昇し
た。

・記憶に新しい2022年は、ロシアによるウクライナ侵攻とＦｅｄの利上げが相俟って、株安・
債券安に見舞われた１年であった。株価は年初から軟調に推移し、S&P500は年初に付けた高
値から10月安値まで25.4％下落、NASDAQは2021年11月の高値から2022年12月の安値まで
36.4％の下落となった。ここでも転機となったのはＦｅｄの金融政策であり、株価が底を打
った2022年10-12月期において、市場の中心的な関心はＦｅｄの利上げ幅縮小（引き締め度合
いの軟化）であった。事実、2022年12月ＦＯＭＣの利上げ幅はそれまでの75bpから50bpに縮
小し、その後2023年２月１日のＦＯＭＣでは25bpに縮小した。市場参加者が利上げ打ち止め
を先取りし、株価が底を打った形であった。

・2026年の株価下落もやはりＦｅｄの利上げ局面で発生している。上述したように金利上昇
は、資金調達コストの増加を通じてＡＩ投資そのものを減速させ得るほか、（安全資産であ
る国債利回りの上昇によって）株式の相対的な魅力を低下させ、株価に打撃となる。投資家
が業績下振れに対する懸念を抱く状況下、安全資産である国債利回りが５％超だったら、国
債を選好する投資家が増えることは自明であろう。

・その点、利上げ局面入りのきっかけとなった原油高が落ち着きつつあることは朗報。米中間
選挙で民主党が躍進したことはイラン情勢の早期好転に資すると考えられ、このことは原油
価格の低下を通じてインフレ圧力を緩和し、Ｆｅｄの利上げ観測を後退させる。もちろん資
産価格に対するインパクトは株高・金利低下であり、これは2026年１～２月の環境が戻るこ
とを意味する。現在、市場を覆っているＡＩに対する先行き不安は、原油価格が低下すれ
ば、Ｆｅｄの利上げ打ち止め観測を介して、急速に後退すると予想する。

十一月 16, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 1

線上法人說明會重點摘要

亞泥 
(1102 TT, NT$35.4, 未評等)

焦點內容 
1. 1Q-3Q26台灣水泥需求年減 13%，主要受房市信用管制及土方新制影
響，建商推案與施工進度放緩。惟自 3Q26 末起需求跌幅逐步收斂，
隨房貸及購地限制鬆綁訊號釋出、土方清運量能恢復，加上科技廠辦
建設支撐，公司認為市場最差時期已過。 
2. 1Q-3Q26 中國水泥需求年減約 10%，全年預估下滑 8-10%，惟亞泥
中國前三季銷量僅年減 7%，優於整體市場。3Q26 受煤價上漲影響，
水泥噸毛利由人民幣 17 元降至 7 元。展望 4Q26，隨煤價漲勢趨緩、
售價調漲及錯峰生產改善，噸毛利有望回升。 
3. 嘉惠電廠二期增購協議 2026 年 10 月底正式生效，發電調度比率由
30-50% 提升至 50-70%，以區間中位數估算，發電量可望增加約
50%，自 4Q26 後半季開始貢獻，2027 年效益將更為顯著。 
結論 
3Q26 兩岸水泥業務受需求疲弱及煤炭成本上漲拖累，但電力事業提供穩
定獲利，加上權益法投資收益成長，部分抵銷水泥本業衰退對整體獲利的
影響。儘管最差狀況已過，利潤率及獲利回升空間有限將壓抑股價動能。

台灣水泥營運概況 
 3Q26 台灣水泥營收年減近 15%，主要受市場需求疲弱影響出貨，
煤炭成本上漲亦壓縮獲利。由於上半年仍有低價煤炭庫存緩衝，成
本壓力自第三季起逐步反映，導致毛利率及營業利益同步下滑。 
 1Q-3Q26 台灣水泥市場需求年減 13%，主要受到房市信用管制及
年初土方新制影響，導致建商推案趨於保守、施工進度放緩。 
 自 3Q26 末起，台灣水泥市場需求跌幅逐步收斂，六都房屋買賣移
轉棟數截至 10 月已回升至持平水準，政府亦釋出房貸及建商購地
限制鬆綁訊號，土方清運量能逐漸恢復。儘管住宅需求仍偏弱，工
業區及科技廠辦建設持續提供支撐，公司認為台灣水泥市場最差時
期已過。 
 台灣煤炭主要自澳洲採購，近期成本上漲主要反映國際煤價走高，
惟受到進口水泥競爭及市場結構影響，公司難以因短期燃料價格波
動頻繁調漲售價，後續仍需與客戶協商成本轉嫁。 
 公司預估 2026 年台灣水泥業務碳費支出約 5,400 萬元。由於獨立
發電廠僅需針對廠內自用電繳納碳費，嘉惠電廠負擔極低，因此碳
費支出主要來自水泥業務。

### Page 2

台灣 亞泥

十一月 16, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 2

中國水泥營運概況 
 中國水泥需求持續處於下行週期，1Q-3Q26 全國需求年減約 10% 
至 11.3 億噸。受兩岸水泥市場需求疲弱影響，亞泥同期合併營收
年減 5.2%至 504.1 億元。 
 3Q26 中國水泥需求年減約 11%，華東地區出貨亦受強降雨影響。
公司預估 2026 年全年需求年減 8-10%，惟亞泥中國前三季銷量僅
年減約 7%，表現優於整體市場，市佔率維持穩定；2027 年噸毛
利預期與 2026 年水準相近。 
 3Q26 中國水泥平均售價季增人民幣 4 元至每噸 189 元，惟漲幅不
足以抵銷煤炭成本上升壓力。受產地安檢、檢修及冬季備貨需求影
響，煤價升至每噸人民幣 830 元 (去年同期 640 元)，導致水泥噸毛
利由人民幣 17 元降至 7 元 (已排除資產減損影響)。 
 展望 4Q26，隨煤炭供給恢復、煤價漲勢趨緩，搭配水泥業者調漲
售價及錯峰生產改善，公司預期噸毛利將較 3Q26 回升，惟仍面臨
煤炭成本壓力。 
 亞泥中國持續推動產能置換，關閉湖北亞東三條舊有產線，將產能
配額轉移至黃岡二線，預計 4Q26 投產，以提升生產效率及改善成
本結構。3Q26 因認列一次性舊產線資產減損人民幣 1.2 億元，導
致營業利益下滑。 
 中國水泥產業中長期預期維持 L 型底部盤整，隨碳交易制度逐步推
進，將擴大業者間成本差距，有望加速落後產能退出並改善產業供
需結構。惟 2026 年碳配額仍相對寬鬆，短期成本影響有限。 
 3Q26 中國熟料配合錯峰生產暫停外銷；骨料受需求疲弱及華東礦
山產能釋放影響，價格與毛利率同步下滑，惟毛利率仍約 20%，
優於水泥業務。貿易業務拓展海外分銷，已帶來穩定獲利貢獻。

電力事業 
 嘉惠電力 3Q26 營收年增約 17%，主要反映天然氣成本上漲帶動售
電價格調升，惟燃氣採購成本同步增加，使營業利益由去年同期
7.8 億元降至 7.3 億元。 
 嘉惠二期增購協議 2026 年 10 月底生效，發電調度比率由 30-50%
提升至 50-70%，以區間中位數估算，發電量可望增加約 50%，預
計自 4Q26 下半季開始貢獻，2027 年獲利貢獻將更為顯著。 
 嘉惠一期獲利仍受天然氣價格波動影響，二期則採類似電力代工模
式，天然氣採購成本可透過售電價格轉嫁，因此獲利主要取決於實
際發電調度量。 
 隨台灣 AI 及半導體產業帶動用電需求成長，公司將透過提高嘉惠
二期發電調度量，擴大電力事業獲利貢獻。

### Page 3

台灣 亞泥

十一月 16, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 3

不銹鋼及其他業務 
 遠龍不鏽鋼業務受市場需求疲弱、庫存調整及原料價格波動影響，
營運持續承壓；由於業務以冷軋及裁切加工為主，產品附加價值及
毛利率相對較低。 
 海外市場方面，新加坡水泥業務佔整體營收比重有限，雖然下半年
需求較上半年改善，但受去年同期高基期影響，年增率略為下滑，
整體營運維持平穩。

3Q26 財報 
 3Q26 營收 177.5 億元 (YoY -0.8%)，毛利率 9.8% (YoY -6.9 個百
分點)，主要受到兩岸水泥需求疲弱及煤炭成本上漲影響，本業獲利
承壓。 
 3Q26 本業獲利疲弱，主要仰賴業外收益支撐，其中權益法認列投
資收益年增 40.2%至 16.0 億元，加上股利收入 5.5 億元，部分抵
銷金融資產評價損失約 6.1 億元，惟獲利仍年減 36.7%至 24.0 億
元，換算每股盈餘為 0.68 元 (YoY -36.4%)。 
 3Q26 權益法投資收益成長主要受惠遠東新世紀處分土地資產帶來
一次性獲利，以及裕民航運受地緣政治影響、散裝航運運力縮減帶
動運價上漲，抵銷山水水泥因中國水泥景氣低迷造成的負面影響。 
 3Q26 營收部門別佔比：台灣水泥 29.6%、中國水泥 27.7%、電力
33.1%、不鏽鋼 3.2%、其他業務 6.4%。 
 受黃岡二線建設帶動，資本支出有所增加，惟公司整體自由現金流
仍維持正數。 
 公司股利配發率提升至 88%，優於同業水準，隱含股息殖利率約
5.7%。 
 3Q26 ROE 為 5.4% (對比 2025 年 4.9%)。

蔡欣妤 
 Lily.tsai@ctbcsis.com

光學眼鏡 
大學光 (3218 TT)

維持評等 
中立(Neutral) 
營運持續回穩 
⚫ 3Q26營收 YoY+13%；單季 EPS維持 3元以上水準 
⚫ 光學事業成長強勁；EVO ICL佔比持續提升 
⚫ 台灣 2027年擴點升級為主；中國新眼科醫院預計 1Q27開幕 
3Q26營收續雙位數成長：大學光 3Q26營收 11.6億元(YoY+13.3%)；
毛利率 57.3%，與上季大致相當，營業費用率 28.0%偏高，主要受暑
假旺季，行銷預算投入較多，且隨光學業績成長，發放的薪資獎酬增
加。業外已於上季處分全數美債 ETF，免除業外損益大幅波動之干擾，
目前以利息費用為主。歸屬母公司淨利 2.72億元，單季 EPS 3.21元。
中國大陸市場方面，2Q26末買回外部股東剩餘 30%股權，目前 100%
持股；3Q26認列虧損 1,100萬元，較上季虧損收斂，既有店達損平，
主要是新據點建置投入費用。 
光學事業成長強勁；屈光手術 3Q26跌幅收窄：依事業別，3Q26光學
事業為主要成長動能(YoY+15%)，兒童近視防控與高階功能性鏡片需
求持續強勁。醫學事業方面，今年公司整體屈光手術量受大環境影響
衰退 10%，然優於市場的衰退 20%，3Q26跌幅收窄，與去年大致持
平；EVO ICL目前佔比已從上季的 5~10%，提升至 12~15%，公司於
4Q26推出新一代 EVO ICL，升級重點在於光學區變大，以大幅降低
夜間眩光與光暈，目前售價從推廣價回歸正式定價，管理層表示參考
鄰近國家滲透率，後續 EVO ICL有望提升至 20%以上。老花及白內障
業務則受惠人口老化趨勢維持穩健。 
台灣 2027年以擴點升級為主；中國指標眼科醫院目標於 1Q27開幕：
通路布局方面，台灣 2026年完成了 6間既有據點的升級，以及新增 2
家合作據點；2027年將以搬遷升級與原店擴大為主，以提升客容量，
此外有 1~2個新據點洽談評估中但尚未確定。中國大陸市場部分，興
建中的江蘇太學眼科醫院預計於 2027 農曆過年前後展開試營運。中
國眼科市場於 3Q26擺脫上半年的衰退，呈現微幅成長，但主要來自
客單價驅動，整體手術量仍未完全恢復；管理層認為中國營運已築
底，但消費市場的復甦力道仍需審慎觀察。 
在 EVO ICL滲透率提升，及中國新醫院加入營運的帶動下，2027年
營收有望重返雙位數成長；然考量主要的屈光市場已步入成熟期，因
此維持中立評等，目標價 140元(2027預估 EPS x 10倍 PER)。風險因
子：EVO ICL銷售增幅、屈光手術市場、中國大陸市場變化。

Document 632: 如何定价“政府债取消免税”

Source PDF: all report pdf/如何定价“政府债取消免税”.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

政府债利息所得税免税取消的讨论因央⾏三季度货币政策执⾏报告⽽再度升温，市场分歧
已从 “ 是否取消 ” 转向 “ 如何定价 ” 。华泰研究判断基准情形为新⽼划断、地⽅债先⾏，预计新
发 10 年期政府债收益率上⾏ 10-20BP ；国盛证券则提出 “ 税负分摊法 ” ，测算⾸年冲击中枢
约 9.1BP ，认为 40BP 是极端边界⽽⾮中枢。
摘要：
11 ⽉ 11 ⽇，央⾏发布三季度货币政策执⾏报告，设专栏《国际利率⽔平⽐较》，其中明确指出 “ 国
债免征所得税使纳税投资者愿意接受低于应税债券的收益率 ” ， “ ⽐较利率时需还原税收因素的影
响 ” 。
这⼀表态，让政府债利息所得税免税取消的预期再度升温。政⾦债、⼆级资本债、永续债、信
⽤债随即出现不同程度的调整。消息发布后， 10 年国债中债估值单⽇上⾏ 0.7BP ，政⾦债调整幅
度更为明显 ——11 ⽉ 11 ⽇⾄ 13 ⽇， 10 年国开债收益率累计上⾏ 7.5BP ，与国债利差由 5.2BP ⾛阔
⾄ 11.0BP 。11 ⽉ 16 ⽇今⽇，国债期货普遍⾛弱， 30 年国债期货跌超 0.5% ， 10 年期国债期货跌

0.13% 。
⽽这不是央⾏第⼀次表态。 2025 年⼀季度货币政策执⾏报告曾提及税收制度影响国债利率基准
作⽤，约 12 周后，政府债利息增值税即恢复征收。
但值得注意的是，这⼀轮的市场分歧已经发⽣了变化。华泰证券固收团队在 11 ⽉ 14 ⽇发布的研
报中指出，当前讨论的核⼼已从 “ 是否取消政府债利息企业所得税免税 ” ，转向 “ 如何取消、何时
取消、影响多⼤ ” 。
“ 政府债取消免税 ” 讨论再度升温
华泰证券固收团队在研报中梳理了 2024 年以来的时间线： 2024 年 8 ⽉，市场曾出现 “ 监管拟取消
利率债基、信⽤债基免税 ” 的预期； 2025 年 8 ⽉，政府债利息增值税免税被取消，确⽴了 “ 新⽼划
断 ” 的操作先例； 2026 年 8 ⽉⾄今，讨论焦点转向国债、地⽅债利息企业所得税。
此轮讨论的直接导⽕索，是三季度货币政策执⾏报告专栏。华泰研究注意到，央⾏在 2025 年⼀
季度货币政策执⾏报告中曾指出， “ 债券市场税收制度设计也会对债券市场价格形成和国债利率

基准作⽤的发挥产⽣影响 ” ，此后不久，政府债利息增值税免税即宣告取消。
“ 央⾏的表态与政策落地存在先后关系。 ” 华泰研究在报告中写道。
按 25% 税率还原， 2026 年 11 ⽉ 13 ⽇ 10 年国债收益率 1.61% 对应的应税等价收益率约 2.15% ，⾼
于 10 年国开债收益率 1.72% 。华泰研究认为，央⾏推动取消政府债免税主要有四⽅⾯原因：免
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

税影响国债利率基准作⽤的发挥；利率⽐较需还原税收影响；国债收益率曲线在货币政策框架
中的地位上升；以及全额免征国债利息企业所得税在主要经济体中并不多⻅。
调整路径：地⽅债或先⾏，国债等修法
国债的免税依据是《企业所得税法》第⼆⼗六条，取消需要修改这部法律，须经全国⼈⼤常委
会审议；地⽅债的免税依据是财税〔 2013 〕 5 号⽂，属于部⻔规范性⽂件，经国务院批准后由财
政部和国家税务总局发⽂即可调整，⽆需修法，也⽆需公开征求意⻅。
这⼀法律层级的差异，直接决定了调整节奏的差异。
国盛证券固收团队在研报中指出， “ 取消免税，本质上是对两部法律的修改，属于国家⽴法机关
的职权范围 ” ， “ ⾛完整个流程通常也需要数⽉乃⾄更⻓时间 ” 。
华泰研究的判断是：基准情形为 “ 新⽼划断、地⽅债先⾏、国债随修法跟进 ” 。地⽅债调整⽆需
修法，未来数⽉需关注财税部⻔公告；国债调整的关键在于 2027 年度⽴法⼯作计划。
新发政府债收益率： 10-20BP 还是 9.1BP ？
取消免税后新发政府债收益率将上⾏多少，是市场分歧最⼤的问题。

华泰证券从四个⻆度推演新发 10 年期政府债的收益率上⾏幅度。
上限：约 54BP 。 若⾃营机构要求完全传导，维持现有税后收益所需的名义收益率需上⾏ 54BP
（收益率 ×25%/(1-25%) ）； 30 年地⽅债对应的理论补偿为 74BP ，对应的价格损失约 16% 。这是
极端上限。

### Page 3

下限：约 10BP 。 国开债利息需缴纳企业所得税⽽国债免税，⼆者信⽤资质相近， 10 年国开债与
国债利差⻓期被视为隐含税率的反映。当前这⼀利差的隐含税率已远低于法定 25% ，原因在于
公募基⾦等产品层⾯免税的资⾦主导了国开债的边际定价。若新发国债向国开债收敛，新发 10
年国债收益率上⾏约 10BP 。
参照系：增值税新⽼划断经验。 2025 年 8 ⽉增值税调整后， 10 年国债新⽼券利差⽉均约 4.8BP ，
更靠近资管产品 3% 档的理论税差，⽽⾮⾃营机构 6% 档的理论值。这说明应税新券主要由交易盘
定价，税负较重的⾃营机构倾向于持有存量免税⽼券。
综合判断： 华泰证券预计，随着应税利率债规模逐年扩⼤、免税资⾦承接能⼒被稀释，隐含税
率可能回升⾄ 6%-11% （⼤致对应 2021-2022 年⽔平），对应新发 10 年国债收益率上⾏约 10-
20BP ， 30 年国债上⾏约 15-25BP 。
国盛证券则提出了另⼀套框架 —— 税负分摊法。
分析师杨业伟等指出，取消免税的本质是债券体系每年多承担⼀笔确定⾦额的税款，可精确计

算，⽆需假设供需弹性。按 2026 年新增政府债约 26 万亿元、 1.6% 利率计，年利息约 4091 亿元，
25% 税率对应⾸年税负约 1023 亿元。假设由新发政府债、政⾦债、信⽤债等约 112 万亿受影响债
券共同分摊，利率冲击中枢约 9.1BP/ 年。
国盛证券强调， 40BP 是 " 税负完全由新券独⼒承担 " 的极端边界，⽽分摊法下税负由体量约四倍
的整个债券体系共同吸收，单位冲击⾃然被稀释。两份报告从不同⻆度出发，区间下限趋于⼀
致，均认为实际调整幅度将⼤幅低于理论上限。
各⽅如何博弈
财政⽅⾯，华泰研究测算，新⽼划断下取消免税后第 1 年财政净增收约 210 亿元，第 10 年约 1904
亿元。但这⼀收益存在临界点 —— 收益率上⾏超过约 40BP 时，财政净增收转负。

### Page 4

地⽅政府⽅⾯，情况更为复杂。地⽅债利息由地⽅承担，⽽取消免税后新增的企业所得税多归
中央与总部所在地。华泰研究测算，地⽅本级财政的盈亏平衡点不超过 16BP—— 地⽅债收益率
上⾏超过这⼀幅度，地⽅本级财政即由净增收转为净减收。 " 取消地⽅债免税可能加重⽋发达地
区的利息负担 " ，华泰研究在报告中写道， " 多付的利息难以通过税收收回，形成财⼒由⽋发达地
区向发达地区的转移。 "
银⾏保险⽅⾯，是成本的主要承担者。2026 年 9 ⽉末，商业银⾏持有银⾏间市场国债的 70.6% 、
地⽅债的 71.7% 。华泰研究测算，以 10 年国债收益率 1.61% 、⼤型银⾏计息负债付息率约 1.3%
测算，若取消所得税免税，持有新券的税后净贡献将由约 55BP 降⾄约 17BP ，下降约 38BP 。保
险⽅⾯，新发 30 年地⽅债相对普通型⼈⾝险 2.0% 预定利率上限的税后利差，将由约 58BP 收窄⾄
约 6BP 。

为何政⾦债和信⽤债跌得更多？
11 ⽉ 11 ⽇⾄ 13 ⽇， 10 年国开债收益率累计上⾏ 7.5BP ， 10 年国债收益率仅上⾏ 1.6BP ， 10 年国
开债与国债利差由 5.0BP ⾛阔⾄ 11.0BP 。
国盛证券对此的解释是： “ 它们在为税收冲击进⾏补涨式定价，或者说，在为过去过度压缩的利
差 ' 还债 ' 。 ”
逻辑在于：政⾦债、信⽤债本⾝的税收政策没有任何变化，但当作为定价锚的政府债新券利率
因征税预期上⾏时，这些品种与新券的相对性价⽐被动恶化，原本已极薄的利差不⾜以覆盖锚
上移带来的重估压⼒，于是引发跟随上⾏。
华泰研究则从利差结构的⻆度分析了各品种的⾛向：

### Page 5

期限利差⽅⾯， 30 年与 10 年国债利差⾯临⾛阔压⼒ —— 取消免税所需的名义收益率补偿与收益
率⽔平成正⽐，超⻓端久期更⻓，完全补偿对应的价格损失约为 10 年国债的 3 倍。
新⽼券利差⽅⾯，存量免税⽼券的稀缺性上升，新⽼券利差将在增值税税差的基础上进⼀步⾛
阔。
信⽤利差⽅⾯，信⽤债与国债的利差将收窄 —— 税收利差消失，向信⽤债与政⾦债的利差收
敛。
铁道债则是个例外 —— 铁道债利息减半征收企业所得税，不受本次可能的税收调整影响，取消
政府债免税后，铁道债相对国债由税收劣势转为税收优势。
机构⾏为将如何重塑
取消免税后，银⾏、保险与公募基⾦的配置逻辑将出现分化。
华泰研究测算，银⾏⾸年多缴企业所得税约 321 亿元，相当于 2025 年商业银⾏净利润 2.38 万亿元
的 1.3% ；按线性替换的简化假设，约 11 年后升⾄ 3084 亿元左右（ 13.0% ）。
银⾏配置⾏为的变化或包括：存量免税⽼券的稀缺性上升，银⾏持有⽼券⾄到期的意愿增强；
参与新发政府债⼀级投标时，要求更⾼的发⾏利率；超⻓政府债的配置意愿进⼀步下降；在利

率债内部，政⾦债与铁道债的相对价值上升。
对于保险⽽⾔，偿⼆代下的资本占⽤限制了向铁道债、政⾦债和信⽤债的平移。华泰研究认
为，保险 “ 更可能提⾼超⻓政府债的买⼊利率要求 ” 。
公募基⾦⽅⾯，华泰研究指出了⼀个值得关注的问题：取消政府债免税后，⾃营机构经由公募
基⾦持有 10 年国债的税后收益率较直接持有⾼约 42BP ， “ 税收扭曲由免税券种与应税券种之间，
转移⾄直接投资与基⾦投资之间 ” 。

### Page 6

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
“ 需要关注公募基⾦分红免税问题何时提上⽇程。 ” 华泰研究在报告中写道， “ 若取消政府债利息免
税，为避免税源经由基⾦渠道流失、维持税制的⼀致性，机构取得的基⾦分红免税安排可能成
为政策调整的下⼀个⽅向。 ”
关键时间节点
华泰研究梳理了需要关注的时间节点：未来数⽉，需关注财政部、国家税务总局是否发布地⽅
债利息企业所得税调整公告； 2026 年 12 ⽉下旬，全国⼈⼤常委会会议是否对征管法修订草案进
⾏⼆审； 2027 年 3 ⽉，全国⼈⺠代表⼤会会议政府⼯作报告与预算报告的相关表述； 2027 年 4-5
⽉，全国⼈⼤常委会年度⽴法⼯作计划是否列⼊企业所得税法修改。
“ 货币政策执⾏报告的表态与政策落地之间存在时差。 ” 华泰研究写道， “2025 年 5 ⽉ 9 ⽇⼀季度货币

政策执⾏报告发布，约 12 周后的 8 ⽉ 1 ⽇，财政部和国家税务总局即公告恢复征收政府债利息增
值税。 2026 年 11 ⽉ 11 ⽇三季度报告专栏再次讨论国债免税，机械参照上述时差，未来数⽉是需
要关注的窗⼝，但所得税调整⽐增值税复杂，实际时差可能更⻓。 ”

Document 646: 宏观经济2027年度投资策略：AI浪潮下中美经济的增长路径

Source PDF: all report pdf/宏观经济2027年度投资策略：AI浪潮下中美经济的增长路径.pdf
Report source: 开源证券
Date: 2026-11-15
Pages: 40
Status: ok

### Page 1

AI浪潮下中美经济的增长路径
宏观经济2027年度投资策略
姓名 潘纬桢（分析师）
证书编号：S0790524040006
邮箱：panweizhen@kysec.cn
姓名  沈美辰（分析师）
证书编号：S0790524110002
邮箱：shenmeichen@kysec.cn
2026年    11月    15日
证券研究报告

### Page 2

核心观点
➢ 中期选举之后：美国政策的变与不变
民主党在中期选举大获全胜，顺利拿下众议院与参议院的控制权，特朗普在接下来的2年时间内，施政或面临一定掣肘。但我们认为，实际变化可能不大：
1.总统可以依靠大量的行政令来实现自己的政策意图，这一点在特朗普第2任期内尤为显著，其发布的行政令数量远超其前任的3任总统；
2.分裂政府下，美国财政扩张或受到一定限制，但无需过度担忧。美国财政支出中社会保障占比较高，因此两党均不会大幅削减支出，避免触怒选民；
3.民主党的AI严监管方案短期或难有效成型。民主党要求对AI进行严监管以及暂缓数据中心建设，更像是一个选战议题， 目前并无有效共识和确切路径；
4.美伊之间谈判条件分歧明显，军事冲突风险并未消除。由于谈判条件分歧明显，特朗普可能会对伊朗进一步采取军事打击，迫使伊朗接受美国的条件。
➢ 通胀波动与AI投资仍将是美联储加息、美国经济的关键变量
1. 油价与通胀波动或仍是美联储2027年加息的关键变量。由于通胀反弹，美联储9月加息一次。10月虽暂停加息，但后续或将继续加息。2027加息的决定

因素，或仍在油价与通胀的波动上。劳动力市场以及美联储的长期改革举措或影响较小。基准情形下，我们认为2026年12月、2027年3月或各有一次加息。
2. AI相关投资对美国经济、通胀影响仍在提升。美国大型科技公司的大规模资本投资，使得投资正在成为美国经济增长的重要增量与变量，对通胀也有一
定的抬升作用。数据上看，2025Q4-2026Q1期间AI相关的投资贡献了美国GDP同比增长的约30%。AI投资正成为美联储加息、美国经济的关键变量。
➢ 中国经济展望：内需温和修复，财政有待加码
1. 2026年外需和新兴产业托底，内需修复仍有分化。 前三季度实际GDP同比增长4.6%，预计四季度增长约4.5%，全年增长约4.6%。AI与机电产业链带动
出口和工业生产，设备更新支撑部分制造业投资。地产开发和传统基建仍偏弱，消费则表现为服务相对有韧性、耐用品需求偏弱。
2. 2027年出口增速回落，基建和制造业投资有望转正。 电子和设备出口仍有支撑，但2026年下半年高基数将压低美元出口同比。跨年财政资金和专项债支

持在建项目续建，设备更新与电子、电气设备投资带动制造业回升。地产投资降幅有望收窄，但销售改善传导至拿地和新开工仍需时间，开发投资继续拖
累总量。
3.消费温和提速，财政支出有望前置。 随着就业和收入逐步修复、促消费政策接续，预计2027年社零多数月份同比增长约2%—3%，汽车、家电等耐用品
的修复幅度仍取决于收入预期、补贴节奏和住房需求。财政延续托底，年初看结转资金和新增专项债能否及时拨付，年中看项目开工和施工能否跟上。
➢ 风险提示：国内政策不及预期、美国经济超预期衰退、关税反复超预期。

### Page 4

民主党横扫两院，特朗普政府陷入“跛脚状态”1.1
• 民主党拿下参众两院，特朗普后2年执政将受到一定约束。从目前中期选举的结果来看，民主党在本次中期选举实现了比较罕见的横扫，不仅同
时拿下了参众两院，且胜选的席位数也较多，这意味着特朗普在后续2年的执政中，无论是财政预算的通过（众议院职责）、人事任免权（参议
院职责）都将会受到一定的约束，内政方面的政策将面临较大的制约。
• 除去施政受阻之外，特朗普可能还将面临民主党国会的多项调查。诸如家族的部分商业行为、加密货币交易甚至于部分政策的法律依据等等，
都将会接受民主党的调查。甚至于再次受到弹劾，届时或对美国政策与市场情绪产生一定的扰动。
图：民主党在参议院取得胜利
资料来源：AP、开源证券研究所
图：民主党在众议院赢得超过250席

### Page 5

民主党横扫两院，特朗普政府陷入“跛脚状态”1.1
• 但民主党并未拿下2/3以上席位，这意味着总统行政权仍然有较大的发挥空间。虽然民主党在国会立法层面对特朗普会形成一定制约，但在美国
当下的政治框架中，总统行政权依旧很大。历史上来看，美国总统的行政权已经历了较为明显的扩张。根据布鲁金斯学会的统计，美国国会已
授予总统超过 120项紧急权力，其中许多赋予总统广泛的自由裁量权。
• 事实上，在特朗普第二任期，其对总统行政令的使用已较前几任总统有非常大的提升。截至2026年10月底，特朗普共签署了292项行政令，超过
前3任总统任期内的全部行政令数量。如果加上其第一任期内签署的220条行政令，特朗普执政对行政令的依赖程度远较其他总统要高。
数据来源：Federal Register、开源证券研究所（T1表示总统上任第一年，数据做累加处理。）
图：特朗普的总统行政令数量较前3任总统都要多

### Page 6

分裂政府下，美国财政扩张或受到一定限制，但无需过度担忧1.2
数据来源：Wind、开源证券研究所（虚线柱状图为中期选举后第2年财政支出）
• 众议院掌握美国财政的拨款权，分裂政府下特朗普的财政扩张可能受到一定限制。特朗普政府2024年再度就职后，由于掌握参众两院，在财政上仍
然保持了较高的支出和赤字率（2025-2026年赤字率均超过5%），理论上民主党掌握众议院后特朗普政府的财政扩张会面临一定约束。
• 但实际上自2008年金融危机后，美国财政呈现出明显的易升难降的局面。这背后的原因在于，美国财政支出中社会保障、医疗保险的占比较高，两
党都不敢大幅度的削减相关支出，以避免触怒选民。因此即使民主党掌握参众两院，美国财政大概率也不会出现明显下降。
图：美国中期选举后的财政支出与赤字并未出现明显下降

### Page 7

分裂政府下，美国财政扩张或受到一定限制，但无需过度担忧1.2
资料来源：美国众议院、开源证券研究所
• 特朗普政府可能利用未来2个月的窗口期推进2027财年预算法案的通过。下一届国会正式就职还需要到2027年1月3日，特朗普可能将利用剩余的2个
月时间，来推动2027财年的财政法案通过。届时两党或将进行较为激烈的博弈。随着12月11日临时拨款到期，美国政府有一定可能再度出现关门。
但从民主党众议院领袖杰弗里斯（Hakeem Jeffries）胜选后的发言看，也没有出现可操作的削减赤字措施。
• 特朗普及共和党可能还会尝试再度提高美国的债务上限。虽然美国的债务上限在OBBBA后被提升至41.1万亿美元，但由于财政赤字较高，BPC预
计最晚在2027年夏季又将达到新的债务上限，为了避免后续受制于人， 特朗普政府可能会选择利用 2 个月的窗口期来推进债限的进一步提升 。

### Page 8

民主党的AI严监管方案短期或难有效成型1.3
• 民主党目前提出的AI监管方案较为混乱，对AI监管的方
向还不明确，所提法案也基本都处于暂停状态。虽然民
主党成功夺取参众两院，且选前很多民主党人表示要对
AI强监管，但9月份民主党在众议院正式提出的《禁止人
工超级智能法案》（编号H.R.10538 / S. 5493）、在参议
院正式提出的《AI风险管理与安全法案》（编号S. 5576）
均停留在委员会提交阶段，暂未有进一步行动。此外，
民主党目前也没有提出得到全党认可的AI监管规则与方
向，我们认为，由于AI发展速度较快，监管规则难以在
短期内形成有效共识，亦难形成明确有效的方案。
• 即使民主党内部能够达成相对一致的AI监管方案，如果
非常激进亦或是对AI发展有较为明显的负面冲击，特朗
普大概率会选择行使总统否决权。
• 两党目前对数据中心造成的成本提升问题，有一定的共
识。两党均认为目前大规模的数据中心建设导致了普通
中小企业和居民的电费出现明显上升，导致了可负担性
下降的问题。但目前两党对如何就这些费用进行分担，

尚缺乏共识，后续或将进一步讨论。

### Page 9

民主党的AI严监管方案短期或难有效成型1.3
• 州一级层面，对AI进行严监管以及暂缓数据中心建设，更像是一个选战议题。由于数据中心建设击中了美国选民关于生活成本上升的痛点，美
国民众对此反对意见较高，叠加大量在建或规划中的数据中心位于共和党主导的州（德克萨斯等，主因监管宽松、土地相对便宜），导致民主
党在选举中对此大力抨击。但从我们前述分析来看，民主党对AI监管也并无明确共识，因此这更像是一个选战议题。
• 选举结束后，数据中心建设或将继续，不过建设成本可能会相应提升。州长选举中民主党亦获得了较大的胜利成果，得克萨斯州等传统红州也
被民主党人翻盘。但随着选举结束，新州长上任之后，也要处理税收、就业等民生议题。因此我们认为民主党州长上任之后，长时间大规模暂
停数据中心建设可能性不大，但可能要求科技公司投入资金补贴当地居民，亦或者减少税收优惠，长期看建设成本将上升。当然，不同州的政
策可能会略有差异，要看科技公司、州政府及当地民众的谈判与博弈情况。

### Page 10

美伊之间谈判条件分歧明显，军事冲突风险并未消除1.4
• 美伊再度发生军事冲突的风险并未消除。自2月底美以联合对伊朗发动军事打击，霍尔木兹海峡的通航受阻已持续超过8个月。虽然6月份美伊双
方曾达成一份谅解备忘录，但随后双方小冲突不断，停火处于较为脆弱的状态，霍尔木兹海峡的通航仍不顺畅。
• 特朗普政府有可能通过升级军事打击，来迫使伊朗政府进行谈判。从前期美伊双方公开的谈判条件来看，双方之间的分歧明显，单纯通过谈判
似乎难以弥补。中期选举结束后，虽然共和党遭遇较大失败，但对特朗普来说，进一步军事打击伊朗的国内政治约束亦同时解除。我们认为，
特朗普可能会对伊朗进一步采取军事打击，从而迫使伊朗接受美国的谈判条件。从目前的情况来看，美军不断在伊朗周边集结，双方之间的军
事冲突升级可能性并未显著下降，相关风险仍需防范。

### Page 11

美伊之间谈判条件分歧明显，军事冲突风险并未消除1.4
资料来源：以色列国会、新华社、开源证券研究所
• 以色列选举结果出炉，内塔尼亚胡未获得一半以上席位。以色列议会选举结果在11月4日出炉，无任何一方赢得过半数。内塔尼亚胡所领导的利
库德阵营虽仍是国会最大党团，但未获得组阁权。从目前情况来看，反内塔尼亚胡的正直党获得组阁权，但组阁谈判分歧明显。如果其组阁失
败，以色列有可能需要再度进行选举，在此期间内塔尼亚胡则将继续担任看守总理。
• 内塔尼亚胡或将积极游说特朗普发动军事打击，从而打破国内政治僵局。对内塔尼亚胡来说，如果可以联合美国发动对伊朗的新一轮军事打击，
则反对派将会面临较大的政治压力，大选僵局反而可能会被打破。因此以色列的行动，可能会成为美伊之间再度发生军事冲突的催化剂。

### Page 13

油价与通胀波动或仍是美联储2027年加息的关键变量2.1
• 美伊冲突导致油价大幅上行，美国通胀压力逐渐提升。美伊冲突后霍尔木兹海峡通行受阻，导致美国国内汽油价格上涨较多，整体通胀水平在
能源价格的推动下出现了较为明显的反弹。冲突爆发前，美国汽油价格维持在3美元/加仑以下，但9月份美国汽油平均价格最高超过4.3美元/加
仑。油价高企下，9月美国CPI同比上升3.74%，核心CPI同比上升2.6%，均出现一定程度的反弹，且能源项在其中发挥了较为重要的推动作用。
• 通胀压力不断增加之下，美联储在2026年9月加息一次，为2024年降息开启以来的首次加息。10月份虽暂停加息，但整体加息行动大概率还将延
续。2027加息的节奏及关键变量，或仍在油价与通胀的波动上。劳动力市场以及美联储的长期改革举措或影响较小。基准情形下，我们认为
2026年12月、2027年3月或各有一次加息。
图：美国9月CPI出现较为明显的上行
数据来源：Bloomberg、开源证券研究所

### Page 14

油价与通胀波动或仍是美联储2027年加息的关键变量2.1
• 美国就业市场在高通胀及加息环境下，展现了较为明显的韧性。维持就业市场稳定是美联储货币政策的关键目标之一，若就业市场走弱，则美
联储的政策中心或将转向降息以保护就业。但10月美国新增非农就业16.5万人，较9月增加10.5万人，且8-9月的新增非农累计上修8.4万人，显示
当前美国就业市场出现较为明显的回暖。
• 失业率方面，10月美国劳动参与率录得61.9%，失业率录得4.1%，分别较9月回升、回落0.1个百分点，失业率整体亦处于相对低位。
• 总的来看，美国就业市场韧性体现的较为明显，特别是失业率未走高，对美联储来说较为重要。在此背景下，劳动力市场不会成为美联储加息
的拖累因素。
图：美国10月非农就业出现了一定幅度的反弹
数据来源：Wind、开源证券研究所
图：美国10月失业率出现回落，且维持在相对低位

### Page 15

油价与通胀波动或仍是美联储2027年加息的关键变量2.1
资料来源：纽约联储、开源证券研究所
AI发展对就业可能带来一定的负面冲击，但目前这一因素尚未显
现。
• 近期美国部分科技巨头宣布了自愿裁员计划，AI可能会对美国
就业市场造成越来越严重的负面冲击。10月微软宣布向6万员
工提供自愿离职补偿，AI发展对美国就业市场的负面冲击可能
会越来越明显，引发市场对美国就业市场走弱的担忧。
• 但实际上，美国劳动力市场或处于“供需两弱”的状态，即在
需求端企业不裁员，但招聘需求变少；供给端，由于特朗普的
移民驱逐政策，劳动力供给也逐渐减少，整体处于一个“脆弱
平衡”的状态。根据纽约联储的研究与调查，虽然AI在美国的
扩散速度非常快，但企业更多的倾向于对员工进行再培训，当
然由于效率提升，企业的招聘需求也有所放缓。因此在特朗普
移民政策导致美国劳动力供给减少的情况下，美国劳动力市场
出现了就业岗位减少，但失业率同步降低的景象，这显示美国
劳动力市场虽然走弱，但整体仍相对平衡。
图：企业预计为使用 AI 而接受再培训的工人比例将增长

### Page 16

油价与通胀波动或是美联储2027年加息的关键变量2.1
• 美联储5大工作组成果在短期内或难对美联储决策产生影响。美联储主席沃什在就职后，宣布成立了5个工作小组，涵盖沟通机制改革、美联储
资产负债表机制、对现有数据来源的使用与依赖、转型时期的生产率与就业、美联储通胀框架。按照美联储的介绍，这5大工作小组将独立运作，
并为联邦公开市场委员会产出严谨的研究结论。
• 但从目前的进展来看，除去生产率与就业小组将在11月16日举行听证、资产负债表小组可能在2027年1季度提交成果之外，其他3个小组目前均
无有效行动进展，这意味着其对美联储在2027年的货币政策制定几乎没有影响。
图：5大工作小组目前尚无有效成果出炉
资料来源：美联储、开源证券研究所

### Page 17

油价与通胀波动或是美联储2027年加息的关键变量2.1
• 沃什力推动的5大工作组改革，大多在短期内难有实质性进展。对当前的美联储来说，其主要的工作方式、制度流程已经经历过多次变革，整体
高度规范化，如FOMC会议的召开、议题讨论都需要与会人员投票通过，短期内很难出现较大的改变。
• 短期来看，唯一有可能发生改革的是沟通方式改革。自格林斯潘起，美联储开始稳步提升其对外沟通的方式，政策透明度不断提升。但至鲍威
尔担任美联储主席期间，市场对美联储如此频繁甚至过度沟通的批评一直络绎不绝。因此短期内对美联储的沟通方式进行改革，或最有可能在
2027年实现，但美联储仍需要提供自己的政策反应函数，透明化的沟通方向或很难实质性逆转。
• 市场关心的资产负债表政策改革，短期内充裕准备金制度也难以撼动。事实上，美联储自2008年起的资产负债表扩张主要是应对各类危机所致，
主动缩减资产负债表需要很长时间的尝试与推进，可能的方向或从缩减其资产久期开始。
图：美联储货币政策沟通透明度自1990年代开始稳步提升
资料来源：美联储、《21世纪货币政策》、开源证券研究所

### Page 18

AI相关投资对美国经济、通胀影响仍在提升2.2
数据来源：Wind、开源证券研究所
• 美国3季度实际GDP环比上升3.4%，较1季度回升1.2个百分点，同比增速回落至2.1%。分部门来看，个人消费支出、私人投资、净出口、政府支
出分别拉动2季度GDP环比增长2.52、3.27、-3.32、0.9个百分点。
• 从2026年1-3季度的美国GDP数据来看，最大的亮点在于投资与消费交替成为经济增长的支撑力量，其中消费是美国经济增长的关键基础与韧性
所在，投资则是美国经济的重要增量与变量，推动美国经济走向复苏。这其中最关键的驱动力则在于美国科技公司的大规模资本开支。
• 数据上来看，自2023年后科技公司带动的AI相关投资对美国经济增长的拉动作用较为明显，根据美联储的研究，2025Q4-2026Q1期间AI相关的
投资贡献了美国GDP同比增长的约30%。
图：美国3季度经济增长有所回升 图：美国投资出现了较为明显的加速

### Page 19

AI相关投资对美国经济、通胀影响仍在提升2.2
数据来源：Bloomberg、开源证券研究所（ 2026财年金额为指引预估）
• 美国科技公司目前仍然大规模进行资本开支，且似乎仍计划在2027年继续加码。自2024年以后，美国主要科技公司开启大规模资本开支，用
于建设AI相关基建，最大可能在AI时代获得先发优势。截至2026财年，4大科技公司的全年资本开支指引月7400亿美元，较2025年增长超过
100%。2027年的资本开支指引虽然仍未给出，但似乎仍打算继续增加相关投入，若进一步增加，则对美国经济的推动作用将更强。
• AI基建在提升经济增长的同时，也在抬高美国的通胀。AI数据中心的大规模建设，在推动美国经济增长的同时，由于其会消耗大量能源，
对美国公用基础设施（电网等）造成较高负担，相关费用逐渐上升。根据达拉斯联储测算，在较合理的数据中心建设和利用率情景下，仅居
民电价渠道就可能使 2030年美国总体 PCE通胀高出 0.04–0.13个百分点。
图：美国科技公司进行了超大规模的资本开支 图：数据中心抬升电价，推动美国PCE实现一定提升

资料来源：达拉斯联储、开源证券研究所

### Page 21

2026年增长复盘：外需托底，内需修复偏慢3.1
➢前三季度实际GDP同比增长4.6%，三季度回升至4.5%。预计四季度同比约4.5%，全年约4.6%。出口与新兴产业继续托底，地产和传
统投资拖累仍在。外需可能较9月高位回落，但韧性仍具。投资方面非地产类投资有望温和改善，取决于广义财政资金下达进度和
地方债投向结构改善；地产政策起效需时间，年内或仍是主要拖累。消费同比或小幅回升，去年以旧换新退坡基数走低，但消费
的长变量暂无改善。
➢增长动能改善。三季度实际GDP同比增长4.5%，季调环比增长1.2%，均较二季度提高。第二、第三产业分别增长3.4%、5.2%。
➢修复仍有结构性 。三季度名义GDP同比增长约6%。外需与新兴产业提供支撑，投资和消费的持续改善仍需政策落地验证。
图：三季度实际GDP回升至4.5%
数据来源：Wind、开源证券研究所
图：三季度第二产业增速有所改善
2
数据来源：Wind、开源证券研究所

### Page 22

2026年增长结构：新旧动能分化3.1
➢全年增长预计仍由优势制造、现代服务和出口链支撑。算力与设备需求向上游传导，但地产、建筑与传统基建修复较慢，行业景
气分化延续。AI和出口链继续提供支撑，二季度受扰动的石油、煤炭等行业恢复，商贸、运输及商务服务同步改善，使总量能够
在地产和传统投资仍弱的情况下回升。与此同时，9月消费和部分投资降幅收窄，也降低了内需拖累。
➢出口、工业生产和利润的亮眼表现，部分来自同一条AI及机电产业链，总量增长更需要检验需求来源的持续性。
图：优势产业形成循环，传统链条修复偏慢

### Page 23

生产：新兴制造和现代服务支撑增长3.2
➢前三季度规上工业增长5.3%，高技术制造业增长14.3%。预计四季度出口交付和电子链仍有支撑，全年生产端表现强于国内商品
需求。9月工业增加值同比增长5.5%，较8月加快0.3个百分点。采矿、公用事业改善，制造业增速小幅放缓。
➢新兴产业仍是支撑。高技术制造业增长15.3%，增速高位回落。电子、专用设备产能利用率同比提高，工业整体利用率仍偏低。
➢服务业同步修复。9月服务业生产指数增长4.8%，较8月加快0.7个百分点，信息服务、商务服务保持较快增长。

### Page 24

制造业投资：设备更新托底，电子链领先3.2
➢ 2026年前三季度制造业投资下降2.1%，电子设备制造投资增长9.6%。预计四季度设备更新延续，全年制造业投资仍偏弱，增量集
中在电子等优势行业。
➢ AI产业链相关投资较强。计算机通信电子设备制造投资增长9.6%，较前8个月加快1.5个百分点。电气机械投资也有所改善。
➢设备更新保持较强支撑。设备购置投资增长9.7%，增速加快0.4个百分点。汽车、化工投资仍降，关注修复能否扩散。
图：设备工器具购置增速继续走强，制造业或迎来拐点

### Page 25

基建：项目为主要制约，新旧依然分化3.3
➢前三季度基建投资下降4.2%，信息传输业投资增长30.8%。四季度资金到位有助于收窄降幅，项目开工和施工进度决定全年改善
幅度。新旧基建表现分化。前三季度基建投资下降4.2%，9月单月同比降幅由-6.9%缩窄至-5.5%，边际改善趋势已现。结构上算力
新基建是亮点。信息传输业投资增长30.8%，道路运输、水利、公共设施管理等传统领域仍弱。
政策加快推动项目落地。面对项目约束，政策的针对性解决方案逐步落地。9月28日国常会提出“加大宏观政策逆周期调节力度”、
“加紧加力推动各项政策落地落实”、“ 推动‘十五五’规划、‘六张网’实施方案等明确的重大工程项目尽早开工建设”，推动
项目加速。9月专项债发行显著加速，达到全年最高点，10月财政部下达专项债务结存限额2500亿元，分配给有实际项目资金需求地
区，体现形成实物工作量的紧迫性。后续投资修复关键取决于项目。

均值为147.2万吨，较9月下降1.4%、同比下降6.0%；月底虽恢复至150万吨，仍未超过9月高点。同期，全国水泥出库量、磨机运
转率及发运率有所回升，反映建筑施工总体需求边际修复，但基建专项用料和沥青开工指标尚未同步走强。资金端，样本建筑工
地资金到位率持续改善，但仍低于去年相近时期。

7
➢预计电网、水网、地下管网等存量改造项目提供底盘，算力网贡献结构性增量。年度投资取决于项目清单、资本金和配套融资，
重点跟踪招标、开工和施工进度。
六张网 “十五五”投资 规模 投资增速/景气判断 主要资金来源 2027年边际变化 对经济拉动效果
水网 >6万亿 较“十四五”继续扩张， 但增速不是最高 财政主导：两重、专项 债、中央预算内投资、 政策性金融 加速：2026Q3受资金约束， 2027项目+资金匹配后实物量 有望改善 较强：工程属性最强，直接 拉动建筑、水泥、机械
新型电网 （固定资产投资） 约5万亿+ 明显高于十四五 央企CAPEX主导：国 网、南网+政策性金融 较确定：特高压、配网、储 能、跨省输电进入建设高峰 较强：投资乘数+设备制造， 制造业拉动强于建筑

### Page 28

房地产：销售和开工偏弱，开发投资承压3.4
➢ 2026年前三季度房地产开发投资下降20.6%，新房销售面积下降13.2%。预计四季度局部市场改善向全国开发投资传导有限，全年
开发投资仍明显下滑。9月新旧房成交分化，价格局部企稳。量上，1-9月新房销售面积同比下降13.2%，降幅较1-8月扩大1.1个百
分点，二手房网签面积则同比增长10.7%（1-8月10.6%），新房需求仍弱。价上，9月一线城市二手住宅价格环比上涨0.2%，一线
城市的新房和二手房环比保持正增且同比降幅持续收窄；二、三线价格环比仍有波动，价格企稳尚未普及。市场改善仍集中于二
手房成交和核心城市价格，传导至新房销售及开发投资仍需时间。

### Page 30

2027年展望3.6
➢ 2027年，预计出口增速从2026年高位回落，国内需求则有望温和改善。投资修复主要来自基建和制造业，房地产开发投资仍将拖
累总量。消费增速有望小幅抬升，但修复幅度取决于就业、收入和住房市场能否同步改善。基准情景下，国内需求对增长的支撑
有所增强，能否弥补出口降速带来的缺口，仍取决于财政资金向实物工作量和居民支出的传导效率。
➢ 2027年出口仍有结构性支撑，但同比增速预计将低于2026年下半年。 2026年三季度，美元计价出口同比由7月的23.9%升至9月的
36.3%，抬高了2027年同期的比较基数。我们判断基准情形下海外需求温和增长，主要贸易市场的关税和准入限制不显著升级，电
子、设备等优势产品继续支撑出口。随着2026年高增速逐步进入比较基期，即使2027年出口额仍高于2025年同期，部分月份也可
能出现同比下降。
图：2027年出口部分月份可能出现同比下降

### Page 31

2027年展望——基建投资3.7
➢资金接续带动转正，施工进度决定修复幅度
➢预计基建投资累计同比由2026年末约下降4%转为2027年末约增长3%。修复主要来自两方面。一是上年结转资金和新一年度专项
债支持在建项目续建，已有项目具备较快形成实物工作量的条件。二是电网、水网、地下管网等领域的更新改造，以及算力等新
型基础设施建设，为投资提供增量。2026年下半年的较低基数也有助于2027年累计增速改善。
➢基建回升的关键在于资金能否进入施工环节。债券发行之后，资金还需完成拨付、落实配套融资并进入项目建设。我们判断明年
年初温和转正、年内累计增速逐步抬升。应重点观察专项债投向、项目开工和施工强度。若发行提速而施工指标改善有限，投资
修复可能后移。 图：2027年基建投资或温和修复

### Page 32

2027年展望——制造业&地产投资3.8
➢制造业投资：设备更新或支撑转正，扩产仍集中在优势行业
➢预计2027年制造业投资累计同比回升。设备更新为企业资本开支提供较稳定的支撑，电子、电气设备等行业则有望受益于算力建
设、产业升级和出口需求。2026年制造业投资基数偏低，也降低了2027年恢复正增长所需的新增投资幅度。行业分化仍会约束制
造业投资的整体弹性。
➢地产投资：投资降幅或收窄，销售改善向开发端传导偏慢
➢预计2027年房地产开发投资累计降幅有所收窄。 降幅收窄一方面来自2026年的低基数，另一方面来自核心城市成交可能率先企稳，
以及存量项目续建对施工的支撑。销售企稳到投资回升之间存在时滞。成交改善后，房企通常先改善现金流、推进在建项目和交
付。只有当去化更稳定、库存压力减轻时，新增拿地和开工才更有可能回升。核心城市的成交改善也可能与全国市场表现分化。
7

### Page 33

2027年展望——社零温和提速，耐用品修复取决于收入和住房需求3.9
➢消费：社零温和提速，耐用品修复取决于收入和住房需求
➢预计2027年社零多数月份同比增长约2%—3%，较2026年下半年的低增速温和改善。基准情景假设就业和居民收入逐步修复，促
消费政策延续，餐饮和日常商品零售保持韧性。2026年部分月份社零增速较低，也为2027年同比回升提供基数支持。服务消费有
望继续支撑居民支出，其中餐饮计入社零，旅游、文娱等更广泛的服务消费需结合服务零售额观察。消费修复的上限取决于收入
预期和耐用品需求。

2027年财政展望：年初看资金接续，年中看支出转化3.10

元。考虑到2027年出口增速预计回落、房地产开发投资仍处于负增长，财政仍需支撑内需，财政扩张必要性提高的情况下，狭义赤
字率或仍维持在4%左右，专项债和超长期特别国债额度有望较2026年有所提高。

4 风险提示
国内政策不及预期：若政策出台或执行不及预期，可能会影响我国经济修复弹性。
美国经济超预期衰退：如果美国经济超预期衰退，可能对全球金融市场带来不稳定因素。
关税反复超预期：如果美国关税反复超预期，可能会对我国乃至全球经济造成影响。

证券评级
买入（Buy） 预计相对强于市场表现20%以上；
增持（Outperform） 预计相对强于市场表现5%～20%；
中性（Neutral） 预计相对市场表现在－5%～＋5%之间波动；
减持（Underperform） 预计相对弱于市场表现5%以下。
行业评级
看好（Overweight） 预计行业超越整体市场表现；
中性（Neutral） 预计行业与整体市场表现基本持平；
看淡（Underperform） 预计行业弱于整体市场表现。

所涉及证券能够在该价格交易。
